CHAPTER II WHEREIN SYMPHONY SAM DISCOVERS THAT A TALKIE TALKS TOO MUCH. 

MR. SAMUEL HICKS emerged from the Electrodrome 

Cinema in a somewhat dazed condition. For nearly two hours he had been transported (as his grandfather had before him) to an alien land in which crooks seemed to predominate. 

Fortunately for Mr. Hicks, however, the crooks in his case were celluloid, and though their revolvers cracked with convincing thoroughness, he was consoled by the appearance of several lithe and lissom ladies in the scantiest costumes who sang with ex-

ceeding verve and gaiety that delightful ballad: "I've got a Red-hot Mamma." 

Luckily for Mr. Hicks' peace of mind the memories of his maternal progenitor were extremely vague. 

He had recently resided in a place where women ceased from troubling and men were at their best-or worst-it depends on the view point. 

Symphony Sam Hicks had celebrated his release from his tenth lagging by a visit to his first talkie. 

He was a rubicund, freckle-faced little man with something of the robin's beady brightness of eye and jauntiness of mind. 

Symph, as he was known in crookdom, was a burglar by profession and a musician by inclination. He had earned his soubriquet by his astonishing gift of extracting music from the unlikeliest instruments. 

His rendering of Sibelius' Valse Triste on the morning when Mr. Robert Gudgeon was introduced to that capable craftsman, Mr. Willis, was long remembered at Wandsworth.

Symph had managed to improvise a most excellent rendering of the music with the aid of a tin spoon, a bowl, and his own tuneful whistle.

It was natural, therefore, that on his release Mr. 

Hicks should gravitate towards a sound film. 

The Talkies were born or spawned-during Symph's incarceration. When, therefore, he reached 

Brixton and saw the blazing lights of the Electrodrome announcing that " Broadway Belles ", the old talkie super film, would show thrice daily, he naturally gravitated towards it. All talkies, after nine months' silence, sounded distinctly good to Symphony Sam.

It was half past eleven and the little crook boarded a bus which went Westward. He knew several desirable, or undesirable, clubs in the Soho district. He might very possibly meet Tony the Wop, or Five

Grand George, two of his college chums, of Borstal and Pentonville days. 66 Still humming beneath his breath the theme song of Broadway Belles ", Symphony Sam alighted in 

Piccadilly Circus. 

Police-constable Pheasant, that melancholy man, reflected for the thousandth time that evening that 

Gilbert was not only right but trite when he remarked that "a policeman's lot is not a happy one ".

With a glistening oil cape draped round his ample girth, a bull's-eye lamp at his belt, P.C. Pheasant was engaged in the pleasant task of trying doors that he knew were locked, to see if by any miracle they were open.

It was a monotonous task. 

He walked stolidly along and passed down clubland. He noticed the massive pilasters of the 

Mausoleum club, the gay baroque of the "Field and Country club ", the austere precincts of the QOWI club, and realised regretfully that there was no health in them even if there was wealth. 

A figure, furtive as the shadows, detached itself. from a shadowed wall. The constable was too busy wondering in which convenient doorway he would be able to light a surreptitious cigarette to notice Symphony Sam.

While at the Ville, Symph had learned from his old friend Syd the Screwsman of a lay that would repay his best efforts provided it was handled rightly. 

The old burglar's advice Symphony now treasured as he would the words of a Red-hot Mamma. "Take these 'ere clubs, there's lashin's of oof in 'em. Eff you can't pick up chink there's always kettles and 'arf o' them 'ave got cups all over the place. All you got ter do is ter do a mike over the back an' then-" 2

Symphony Sam chuckled. He was dressed at the moment in a neat grey suit that blended admirably with his background. Since his release from Pentonville he had recovered his usual burglar's kit, designed for him by that prince of cracksmen, Cracky McGirk.

Slowly he reconnoitred the dim and shuttered alleyway which debouched from Park Avenue to the precincts of the "Q" club.

The friend and colleague of his college days at Borstal, Charley the Chat, had often told him that a convenient stack pipe would enable him to achieve wealth beyond the dreams of avarice. 

He stopped, and he looked, and he listened. 

In an adjacent doorway the constabulary bosom heaved with relief. In two hours' time its duty would be done.

Symphony Sam caught the glint of the badge on a helmet as he started to climb the stack pipe. "Poor old flatty," he muttered, skirting the Adam portico of the "Q club.

It was easy enough for him to blend as one with the shadows of the night. There was a dark pool of purple reflected from an arc lamp standard in which he crouched like an agile alligator in a Florida flood.

An oblong of lemon light shone through the darkness and Symphony Sam's artistic soul delighted in the delicate tapestried traceries of the priceless 

Moharum curtains of the " Q" club. 

"Blimey," said Symphony. "It looks as if a spot of the doings is 'idden be'ind these 'ere indications.' 

Under his breath he began to hum the recurring refrain of the talkie he had just seen-" She's a Redhot Mamma ". 

Down below, Police-constable Pheasant, who was a movie fan in his spare time, recognised the air and very slowly and solemnly the constabulary feet accom-

panied by the constabulary lips accompanied the song. 66 

I've a Red-hot Mamma, a Red-hot Mamma, an' she don't mean nothink at all- Whether Constable Pheasant's maternal progenitor was quite as incandescent as she was painted is forever a mystery; but he flashed his bull's-eye upwards, downwards, sidewards and backwards. He had always had a hankering for the movies but luckily for Symphony Sam, he had by this time negotiated the window of the “Q” club.

Very cautiously Symph tip-toed his way through a narrow picture-lined corridor which led to the library of that Satanic club which the Hon. Rupert Gill had invented.

He was so delighted with his progress that a song rose naturally bubbling from his lips. "She's a Red-hot Mamma, knows 'er Alpha Beta

Gamma, an' she don't mean nothing at all-

Professor Quin was standing, feet astraddle in the centre of the Bokhara rug, expounding his latest theory. He glanced at Nigel Cavendish and his bushy eyebrows lifted humorously. CHAPTER IV 

WHEREIN THE BENEVOLENT MR. BUNDY OF MOURNING BRIDE ALLEY BRINGS BALM TO A TROUBLED SOUL. 

THE offices of Mr. Bertram Bundy in "Mourning Bride"

Alley, that quiet little backwater from the roaring traffic of Fleet Street, were eminently, almost painfully, respectable. 

Hardly a sound from the Street of Ink disturbed the calm serenity of that quaint old court that has hardly changed since the days when portly Sam Johnson, accompanied by the faithful Bozzy, dined and wined in the very room in which Mr. Bertram Bundy now sat, a pink, benevolent figure at his Sheraton writing desk.

Situated on the second floor of the grave, austere Queen Anne house, the exterior gave little indication of the charm and luxury within. But then Mr. 

Bertram Bundy disliked ostentation. In manner he was as shy, retiring, and almost as archaic as 

Mourning Bride " Alley itself. "S The tiny silver plate on the lintel of his door was hardly larger than a visiting card, and bore merely the legend: " B. Bundy, (Agent)." "B. 

What he was agent for nobody troubled to inquire. 

Mr. Bundy was accountable to but one man for his actions. That man was as omniscient as a deity and almost as aloof. Mr. Bundy paid no rent, for the simple reason that he nominally owned the entire building. His business must have been select but lucrative, for in the months in which he had been established at Mourning Bride " Alley he received very few callers. 66

His post-bag was meagre, save for a certain crisp morning in January, when a uniformed messenger arrived with a sack of such dimensions that it looked as if a belated Christmas mail had arrived from the world's children to Santa Claus.

The room was furnished with exquisite taste that stamped the owner as a connoisseur of the beautiful. 

Mr. Bertram Bundy was a portly, middle-aged man with a cranium like a very pink egg resting in cotton wool, for the fringe of white hair which lent an added benevolence to his round, good-humoured face was as symmetrical as the tonsure of a priest. 

He wore good clothes of an old-fashioned cut. His high stock collar and neatly-tied cravat of watered silk gave him an appearance that blended perfectly with his surroundings. The only anachronism in that room was the telephone on his desk, and it was perhaps because he realised its discordant note that Mr. Bundy sighed as he lifted the receiver and called up a certain number. "Good morning," he said, pleasantly, and his voice had a tinge of obsequiousness in it as he recognised that of the speaker at the other end of the wire. "The mail this morning is almost double that of yesterday, sir," he remarked. "See that you weed out the undesirables," came back the reply. "You answered those two I selected? 66 

Certainly," replied Mr. Bundy. "I have made an appointment for eleven o'clock to-day," he added, with a glance at the solemnly ticking grandfather clock in the corner. "Good; I shall be along at ten-thirty precisely," came the answer.

A faint shadow marred the serenity of Mr. Bundy's features. 66 

Delighted, sir," he replied, and rang off.

With another sigh he rose to his feet and crossed over to a bookcase in the corner. His next action was paradoxical. While his fingers were outstretched ostensibly to remove a vellum-bound volume, they pushed instead of pulled. There was a click, and the front of the bookcase revolved softly, to reveal a perfectly equipped laboratory with dazzling white enamel walls and two black-topped work benches.

A complicated arrangement of wires and batteries flanked one side of that utterly unexpected room, and a five-valved wireless set stood on a centre table. 

Mr. Bundy, in his old-fashioned suit, looked an anachronistic figure in that miniature temple of 

Modern Science. 

He shivered slightly as his gaze fell on the smooth glazed tiles on the walls and the steel-grilled door beyond. They were unpleasantly reminiscent of a recent environment which B. Bundy, Agent, had endured for five years. 66

CHAPTER VI “THE JOLLY WATERMAN" DOES NOT JUSTIFY ITS NAME. 

SYMPHONY SAM felt as if he were entering a morgue when, shortly before eight, he arrived through the dingy swing doors of the "Jolly Waterman" in

Flite Street, Chelsea. He had received a letter from 

Quin, couched in no uncertain terms, ordering him to be at the public house at 8 p.m. 

A morose-looking man stood with his arms akimbo on the counter, surveying the sporting results. He surveyed the little crook as though it had been a personal grudge.

"Rotten night, mate," he ventured to the surly publican, raising his tankard somewhat shakily to his lips. 66 

Yus," grunted the other, without bothering to look up from his paper. 

Eight o'clock chimed faintly from Chelsea Old

Church, and Symphony started violently as the swing doors opened with a swoosh to admit a tall, gaunt individual clad in a weather-stained ulster with a broadbrimmed felt hat drawn low over his brow, thrusting his face into shadow.

Instinctively he knew that the newcomer was the man he had been told to meet.

He sat down at a near-by table as the gaunt stranger strode up to the bar and ordered himself a whisky and soda in a husky voice. 

His piercing eyes rested for a moment or two on

Symphony's face. Then, with a lop-sided grin, he seated himself beside the little crook and spoke through the corner of his mouth with scarcely any movement of his lips.

Symphony recognised the infallible sign of one who has been in stir. "Your stranger. monniker, Symph? " snapped the 

The little crook nodded. "At ten o'clock there'll be a car outside 'ere. Yer goin' for a little ride," said the other, without any preliminaries. 66 

Symphony shivered. He had heard in Pentonville what going for a little ride" meant in American crook circles.

The man with the broken nose sounded like a Yank. 

When Chicago's gangmen take their victims for “a little ride he knew that those victims never returned. 

Panic seized Symphony. He was going to be

bumped off because he knew too much. He did not know which way to turn. If he reported to the police, he'd be pulled in as sure as fate, and the damning evidence of the finger-prints would railroad him to the gallows. 

He gulped down his beer with an effort. "A little ride?" he echoed feebly. "Sure, that's what I said," snapped the other.

Ask no questions. It's a cinch of a job.” 66 "But I ain't got me tools," stammered Symphony. "You don't need no tools, my bucko," returned the other, with a grim smile. All yer got to do is ter keep yer trap shut, open a window, do a little job, and pop out again. Mind what I'm tellin' yer. Ten o'clock sharp a car pulls up on the other side of the kerb. You walks straight to it, gives yer monniker to the driver, an' hops in. 66 There'll be somebody waiting for yer inside an' he'll tell yer the rest.”

The man with the broken nose drained his whisky at a gulp, closed his thin lips like a steel trap, and without another word, rose to his feet and vanished into the night.

Symphony Sam, for the next two hours, sat like a man in a trance. He ordered drink after drink to try and numb the carking worry that gnawed him incessantly. He was not afraid of a lagging, but the mystery and the grim spectre of the gallows that seemed to loom ahead of him whichever way he turned, had broken the little man's nerve. 

He sighed.

Two hours had never seemed so long in the little crook's memory. The raucous shout of " Time, gentlemen, please," jerked him to his feet, and squaring his shoulders resolutely-the beer by this time had some effect-Symphony Sam's humour,

which never lay long dormant, rose to the surface as he staggered out into the fog. 64 

After drinkin' severil 'earty tankards," he murmured, with a wry smile, "the condemned man walked firmly to the scaffold." 

From the grey mist a sinewy hand shot out and gripped his shoulder, and a voice hissed sibilantly in his ear: "This way, my friend."

The courage oozed clamily out of Sam as he felt himself propelled towards a long black shape on the other side of the road, lithe and evil as a panther with its bright yellow eyes staring through the fog. 66 

Inside, my friend. You are going for a little ride," said the unknown.

Unresisting, the little crook was led into a cushioned tonneau. Came the clang of a door, a grinding of gears, and the car shot forward into the fog. 

The interior of the tonneau was in darkness, but 

Sam knew he was not alone. 

"My impetuous friend," said the cool, mocking voice of the stern-faced man whom he remembered had taken his finger-prints in the "Q" club. “You will listen to my instructions very carefully. Failure to carry them out means death.' 

Symphony licked his dry lips nervously. He felt utterly trapped and cornered. 66 I want you to enter a certain house by a window I shall indicate. When you arrive there you will place a certain object I will give you on a shelf. Leave it there and come out.' 

'Leave it there an' come out," echoed Sam, voicelessly. Was not that what he had done on his disastrous entrance into the "Q" club? He had entered a burglar, and left-a murderer. "Leave it there, and come out with as much speed

as possible. Do not dare to strike a light. I have looked up your records, Hicks; I know you are peculiarly gifted. You do not need an electric torch. You are English, therefore you will not get an electric chair-if-you-will-listen--to-me ! " 

Symphony Sam gulped a little. Something of a craftsman's pride stirred within him. It was true, he had the strange faculty of being able to see in the dark, which was probably why he made such a successful cat burglar. 66 'But look 'ere, guv'nor," he said, he said, hoarsely. "What's the lay? I can crack a crib all right, but- 

"You will crack no crib, my friend, nor will you leave any traces behind you. You have learnt by now, I trust, how damning finger-prints can be. 

Here, take these! "

Something wet, slimy, and clammy touched Symphony Sam's hand in the darkness as the car sped onwards. He gave a strangled cry, and his flesh crawled with horror as though he had touched a snake. 

A mocking laugh came from the interior of the tonneau. "Don't be a fool. Put them on."

With a sob of hysterical relief, Symphony Sam realised that a pair of surgical rubber gloves had been thrust into his moist palm. "Put them on," repeated the unknown. 

Symphony Sam gazed round him.

The fog grew thicker and the car slackened speed. 

But the fog in the mind of Symphony Sam was thick and impenetrable. The cold, dank rubber gloves which he held in his hand emanated the strange sickly odour of iodoform, a hospital smell, a mortuary smell.

He remembered now the last time he had en-

countered that queer perfume, when he had identified the body of Lefty McGurk. How long ago was it? 66 

Put-on-those-gloves ! " said his companion, slowly. 

With a shudder of revulsion, Symphony Sam drew on the ten clammy rubber fingers that were not more cold or clammy than his own.

Outside, the roar of London's traffic bearing homeward-bound theatre-goers and tired toilers, was as remote from the point of view of contact and aid as if he were a ship-wrecked mariner on a raft with nothing but a crystal set listening in to 2LO. 66

Isaid the acid voice. "Most people want to get exactly what they want."

That house is tenanted by a deaf old man with a nephew who at this moment is listening with gusto to the canned voice of Senorita Lucille del Rio in the all-talking triumph, The Sob 

Stuff Sisters'." 

Symphony shuddered. He had heard many juveniles attribute their first step in crime to the movies, but he was the first full-blown criminal who could attribute his downfall to the talkies. 

"It is a very quiet neighbourhood," continued the other, quietly. You will find, as is usual, that there is a back door, a scullery window, and leading to the right of it, a fairly commodious pantry. All I want you to do is to open the window, enter, and place this

Came a click, and the tonneau was flooded with light. 

Symphony Sam started.

Next to him sat a man clad in an Inverness cape and a wide-brimmed black hat. Of his face, Symphony could see nothing, save two malignant eyes and a two-inch strip of sallow skin that appeared between the brim of his hat and the top of a black silk handkerchief tied about the lower part of his face. "This is the room you will enter," said the man, pointing to a roughly-drawn plan on a note-book he held on his knee. On the right here is a path." 

He touched two parallel lines with a well-manicured finger nail.

Here is the back door. To the right of this is the pantry window. It has one hasp which an expert like you can easily unfasten. On the right again is a series of shelves. All you have to do is to place this- 

The light went out, and the impenetrable blackness confused even Symphony Sam's alert and strangely luminous eyes.

Something was thrust into his hand-a hard, pearshaped object with ridges. It was obviously wrapped in some sort of stiff cartridge paper. 

Symphony Sam's heart beat a trifle faster. He had served, during the war, with distinction in the P.B.I., and the words of his tormentor flashed into his mind. "When you have placed it there, clear out quick! 

Don't light a match!' 99 

He ran his fingers, calloused from months of labour stitching His Majesty's mail bags, over the pear-shaped object. It seemed too light for a Mills bomb, and yet The car drew to a standstill and the noise of the

traffic was as impersonal now and as far away as the surge of a far-off sea. 66 

Remember, Hicks," snapped the masked man. 'I will come with you to the door. You will do exactly as I tell you, and nothing else. This is not burglary. You are entering that house and you are leaving something—not taking anything away. This way. 

Fingers as firm as steel closed round the little crook's biceps. There was a click, and the door of the car clanged open, and the little crook found himself on the pavement of a suburban street shrouded in the impalpable grey mist of a London fog.

It was not so dense here as it had been in Chelsea. 

It was more delicate, white, and wraith-like. "Up this path," hissed the man in black. "I have shown you the plan. We will await you in the car here. You can snick back the window-the rest is child's play. Walk on the grass-the gravel crunches."

Symphony Sam was no novice in the art of housebreaking, but never before had he committed a burglary under such precise directions. Almost before he realised it he was piloted down a narrow alleyway which skirted the back of solid suburban residences that, in the impalpable haze of a fog and only then, looked beautiful.

His quick Cockney brain had already memorised the plan, and it was nothing for the agile little cat burglar to scale the low garden wall which was the visible sign of demarcation between "Sandringham ” and its utterly plebeian and foreign, and therefore naughty neighbour, "Sans Souci ".

He dropped to the turf without a sound, and noticed that the house was shuttered and dark. His brain was in a turmoil, but long years of discipline, serving His Majesty on the Western and also the

Home Secretary's front, led him straight for the window, outlined according to plan. 

From his pocket, with a deft gesture, he extracted a long, thin-bladed knife. It was the work of a moment, to a man of Symphony's capabilities, to snick back the hasp of the window. 

The fog grew darker. The little crook was keyed up to zero point. He knew that on the right lay the room with two shelves, on one of which he had to deposit that which he held in his hand. 

Death lurked everywhere. Failure meant the gallows. If he stayed too long, then, thought 

Symphony, death wouldn't! 

Into the darkness he crept. Noiseless as a cat he dropped. He reached out a gnarled hand that fell on something cold, clammy, and unutterably beastly. 

It was a blancmange. "Blarst!" said Symphony Sam. It was the last straw. Bomb or no bomb, the people who dwelt in this house could be made to suffer. 

He was a soldier for the duration, but he was a burglar by profession. Besides, 'adn't 'e been the champion scrounger of the Umpteenth Middlesex?

He left the pear-shaped object on the shelf, and dashed forward into the darkness. "I'll teach the bleedin' blasters to keep their blancmanges abaht," said Symphony Sam Hicks.

With cat-footed tread he reached a luxuriouslyfurnished room. In the light of the dying embers of the fire he caught the gleam of silver on the shelves. 

The liquor he had drunk in the "Jolly Waterman had made him reckless. 

"Blarst old Bolshie Bill in his car. I might as well be 'ung for a sheep as a lamb," thought Symph. 

With quick, practised gestures, his hands reached out for the more glinting and portable objects on the sideboard and with a grin, Symphony Sam stuck

them in his capacious pocket and with his rubber gloves softly re-opened the window and alighted rubber-footed and rubber-handed on the lawn of 

Sandringham ". 

Accacia Avenue slept the sleep of the just-or of the only just. Some of the innate Cockney occultism surged up in the soul of Symphony Sam. The excitement of the moment had grown so intense that he expected the whole house to be blown to fragments. 

With incredible rapidity he vaulted over the garden wall into the fog-laden street.

Home, Trotsky," said Symphony Sam-then gasped.

There was no sign of a motor car, and round the corner steadily, inexorably, came the unmistakable footsteps of a policeman on his beat.

CHAPTER VII INTRODUCES SOME GENTLEMEN OF THE PRESS 

66 

AND THEIR FLIMSY EXCUSE FOR EXISTENCE. 

THE newly-erected and ornate offices of the "Daily Ray," much to the disgust of Fleet Street's oldest inhabitants, occupied the site of two of their favourite pubs and their sausage shops.

In their American concrete whiteness, rearing their five stories skyward, they represented, like sermons in stone, the slogan of their owner, Baron Hovenden: per ardua ad astra "; or, as Bannerhead Bruce, the star crime reporter of that go-ahead journal, remarked in a cynical manner : per para and peerage ". 66 66 

Mr. Bannerton Bruce, or Bannerhead as he was

familiarly known in the Street of Ink because of his uncanny flair for collecting front-page news stories, was the youngest of that picturesque school of journalists who are known personally and not impersonally in the ink columns of their newspapers. 

Somewhere about the time that Symphony Sam was entering "Sandringham ", Bannerhead Bruce, a tall, elegant young man, whose eccentricity was an oblong monocle and a conversation that sounded like a headline, was engaged in one of his favourite pasttimes, that of baiting the news editor, Mr. Hector 

Mac Whiffle. 

He was seated at the moment dangling his long legs negligently on the edge of the news editor's desk, and commenting bitterly as he inspected the flimsies on the spike. 66 99 From the bowels of the vast Ray building came the rumble of the rotary machines. 66 

It was, as Bannerhead Bruce remarked, as if the sky-scraper had a pain in its guts and only Pep and 

Sin could cure it.'

Mr. MacWhiffle chewed up the end of an unlit cheroot which had been given him by an unlucky contributor.

He was a tall, gangling individual, with the high cheek bones of the Gael, and the hard bright eyes of a Scotsman whose ambition to conquer England had been realised. 

His hair was grey, but not with years. The celebrated prisoner of Chillon had an innocuous, almost a festive time, compared with that of the night news editor of the "Daily Ray ".

Hector frowned gloomily as he opened a cablegram from the Hong-Kong correspondent of the "Ray ". 

Dejectedly he pulled at his unlit cheroot. 66 

Another general changed sides. Joined the Salvation Army I suppose, Bannerhead," he grumbled.

Bannerhead Bruce grinned. 

"Poor old Mac, it's a grand life if you don't weaken," he remarked. I notice that there are ten thousand casualties on the Yangtse.' 

"But save for "Och aye," replied MacWhiffle. the fool who put his heid in a gas oven at Rickmansworth, there's no corpse worth chronicling. One man murdered in London is worth more space than fifty dead men in Denmark or ten thousand in China." 

Bannerhead Bruce agreed heartily. "You said it, Mac, it's a dud night.. Not even a statesman has the decency to die." 

Wearily Bruce glanced through the flimsies, mostly chronicling the small beer of journalism, and yawned.

He was a brilliant journalist. All the flotsam and jetsam that drifted into the news room of the "Ray " from the four corners of the earth, there to be winnowed until the chaff was separated from the grain, came under his keen-eyed scrutiny. He had been known to write a double column splash page story on far less promising material than that which festooned Mac Whiffle's file.

He tossed over a photograph of a rather plump and good-looking woman, with a somewhat insipid smile, to which was attached a typewritten caption. "The Whiflet Fotheringay female again,' announced. "Looks like being a good story.'

Bannerhead Bruce, most blasé of newspapermen, glanced wearily at the pile of flimsies on his desk, chronicling everything, from an earthquake in China to a vicar's protest against bridge in Giggleswick. 

It was six o'clock in the evening, and Bruce felt extraordinarily bored. Three days had elapsed since his conversation with Hector MacWhiffle on the ethics of journalism, and in that period no crook had been obliging enough to break the law and give Bannerhead a chance of putting over the news stories for which he was famous.

A printers' devil entered his room in that pertly deferential manner which belongs entirely to the denizens of Fleet Street. 

He ripped open the telegram, and smiled. It was from the Paris correspondent of the "Daily Ray ", announcing the fact that M. Jules Bontemps, the famous jeweller, was arriving that afternoon with a quarter of a million pounds' worth of gems in his possession. 

Bannerhead Bruce's grey eyes grew suddenly alert as he read that message. There was certainly a story in it.

The door of his glass-fronted shanty opened again, and a printers' devil, inkier if possible than the last, entered, and slammed down an envelope on the desk. 

Leaving a rich array of finger-prints on its vellum

surface: "A gennulem 'as left this," he remarked, and with a weary sigh, Bannerhead opened it. 

The envelope had been addressed to the editor of the "Daily Ray ", and it was in no very amiable frame of mind that Bannerhead Bruce extracted its contents. He knew those letters, which were mostly wails about the income tax, or dirges about the dearth of long skirts. 

Wearily he lit a cigarette as he unfolded the letter. 

Suddenly Bannerhead Bruce stiffened. Whoever his unknown correspondent was, he at least had the knack of telling his story in the first three lines. 

The letter ran : "Dear Sir, "To-morrow night Mrs. Whiflet Fotheringay appears at the Frivolity Theatre, in the rôle of Cleopatra, and wearing a tiara kindly lent by M. 

Jules Bontemps, the celebrated Parisian jeweller. 

I dislike this vulgar ostentation, and I shall have great pleasure in not only stealing her tiara but also her magnificent pearl necklace. "Once I have got them it is up to the police to restore them again, but I hope to raise several thousands on behalf of our British hospitals before they tumble to who has done it. Madame "Don't forget this is a challenge.

"CONSTANT READER."

Henry had suggested, he was embarking on a strange and terrible quest.

Symphony Sam, but Bannerhead Bruce, whom he knew slightly in the days when he also was a journalist in Fleet Street. He would have to watch his step, however. It would not be so easy to fool the four shrewd and unscrupulous members of the "Q" club as he had succeeded in besting the amateur conspirators of the Secret Six ". 64 

He came to a sudden grim decision. If these four evil men, conscious of their wealth and prestige, were embarking, out of pure devilment, on a campaign of crime, then he resolved to pit his wits against theirs. 44 There was a zest in the thought. Once again Bill 

Kellaway recaptured the old thrill of playing a lone hand against overwhelming odds. If the four Sons of Satan" in their arrogance, pride, and unscrupulousness, thought they could get away with four perfect crimes, then he, Bill Kellaway, would checkmate them at every move.

The prospect was exhilarating. The danger was considerable, but Bill joyed in battling against heavy odds. One thing was certain. Not by the slightest suggestion or phrase would he express disapproval of their cold-blooded project. The one way in which he could fight and beat the four " Sons of Satan" was by pretending to be in complete agreement with their crimes. 66 

Lucifer, he decided, would out-Beelzebub Beelzebub-on the surface at least. 

In the meantime he would watch and wait until he had obtained sufficient facts and data to enable him to proceed with his fight against the members of that Satanic organisation.

CHAPTER XIII SCOTLAND YARD SUSPECTS. 

INSPECTOR BARKER of Scotland Yard was a worried 

The publication of "Constant Reader's " letter in the Evening Wireless " had had more farreaching effects than he had at first supposed. 66 

Bannerhead Bruce and Mac Whiffle, between them, had worked the stunt for all it was worth. 

Barker foamed at the mouth, especially as Bannerhead suggested that the "Daily Ray" had conducted their own investigations into the threatened robbery at the Frivolity and that they were convinced that it was no mere publicity stunt. 

Barker was seated at his desk in his austerelyfurnished but light and pleasant room overlooking the Thames Embankment. It was a somewhat chill afternoon, and the river was grey and leaden. A brisk East wind whipped flecks of foam on its sullen surface.

Barker gazed out of the window, chewed savagely at his cigar, and gloomily surveyed a fussy little tug as it negotiated Westminster Bridge.

A buzzer rang on his desk, and with a growl he tossed his cigar into the grate. The Chief Constable had summoned him into his presence. Nobody who first saw the bluff, rather bucolic exterior of Chief

Constable Gervie would have taken him for one of the shrewdest members of the C.I.D.

He was bearded, slow of speech and of movement. 

He looked rather like a prosperous farmer in his loosely-cut grey tweeds. It was only when one was arrested by his sharp and piercing grey eyes that one realised that his rustic appearance masked a shrewd and efficient brain. "What're you doing about this Frivolity business, Barker?" asked the Chief Constable, in his slow 

Sussex drawl. 

Barker shrugged infinitesimally. "I don't know what to do about it, sir. I thought of going down myself and taking a couple of men with me, just in case something happened. Personally, I consider the whole thing's a publicity stunt, but it's as well to take precautions, I think, in case it might reflect on us if anything happened." 

H'm!" said the Chief Constable, slowly, stroking his beard. "I'm inclined to take the matter a little more seriously than that, Barker. The Daily

Ray' has a certain reputation to keep up, and unless it believed there was a certain likelihood of something spectacular coming off it wouldn't risk these advance articles and being made to swallow its words to-morrow morning." 

It was about six o'clock on Monday afternoon. 

Mrs. Whiflet Fotheringay was scheduled to appear at the Frivolity that evening "complete mit dam diamants", as Barker savagely remarked to his colleague. He had not taken the "Ray's letter too seriously, but neither had he overlooked the possibility that some crazy and spectacular crook might attempt to gain a little cheap notoriety by the attempt. 66 

Tell me exactly what steps you have taken,” said the Chief Constable.

Barker cleared his throat, and stroked his waxed moustache in a nervous gesture. 66 Well, sir, I took the precaution of interviewing this M. Bontemps fellow," he answered. "It seems to me that he has taken all the precautions they need to take. He himself, accompanied by Benskin, are arriving at the Frivolity at seven p.m. He has the jewels with him in a specially-constructed steel-lined case, and as you know, Big Bill Benskin isn't likely to fall for any funny business. The Fotheringay woman's turn is timed for seven-forty-five." 

The Chief Constable nodded.

Ex-Detective Inspector Benskin had recently retired from the Yard and established himself a successful private detective agency of his own. There was no question of Benskin's probity. He had had a long and honourable record at Scotland Yard. "What does Benskin think of it, anyway? " asked the Chief Constable. 

Barker shrugged. "He is inclined to think that the whole thing's a publicity stunt for the Fotheringay female," he said. 66 Frankly, Chief, I'm almost inclined to agree with him. It looks to me like another stunt on the part of the Ray', and Bannerhead Bruce is hard up for copy. Look at that darned silly rot he wrote about the Tootham burglary with his Chalice of Death' and all that nonsense.' 66 "Ha," said the Chief Constable. Wasn't the inquest on old man Judd being held to-day? What was the verdict?" "The jury "Oh, the usual," shrugged Barker. returned a verdict in accordance with the medical evidence-' Death from botulism'.

At that moment the 'phone bell rang, and the Chief Constable's ham-like hand reached for the receiver.

He listened for a while, occasionally interjecting a monosyllable.

There was a long pause. He clapped his hand over the mouthpiece, and turned to Barker. 66 "Bruce of the Ray' speaking," he announced.

Somebody's just rung through to the Ray' office confirming his letter of Wednesday. He rang from a public call office, and announces his intention of stealing the Antoinette tiara at seven-forty-five tonight. We can't afford to take risks. You'd better go there yourself-take a Flying Squad tender along and see what comes of it. It'll only mean half an hour, anyway," he added. 

Barker gnawed at his moustache. 66 

Righto, Chief, but I'm willing to bet the whole thing's just publicity-either for the Fotheringay woman or Mr. Bloomin' Bannerhead Bruce."

The Chief shrugged, and scanned a telegram from New York, which announced the interesting fact that the notorious Scarface Sheeny was on his way to London, and was wanted for at least three murders in Chicago.

CHAPTER XIV THINGS HAPPEN FRIVOLOUSLY-AND OTHERWISE. 

EX-DETECTIVE INSPECTOR BENSKIN was a tall and melancholy-looking man. Twenty-five years of active service in the Metropolitan Police had given him a distinctly jaundiced outlook on humanity, which three years of private practice on his retirement had served only to confirm.

He listened gravely to that voluble little jeweller, M. Jules Bontemps, while the Frenchman impressed upon him various unnecessary instructions regarding the safety of his jewels.

M. Bontemps was considerably agitated as the time for La Belle Fotheringay's performance drew nearer.

He had had another visit from the dynamic newspaperman of the "Daily Ray ". 

Daily Ray", and it was on

Bruce's advice that he had called in the services of ex-Inspector Benskin.

It was shortly after half past six. M. Bontemps. had obtained the famous jewels from the safe deposit vault, and the two were closeted in the detective's room, hard by Shaftesbury Avenue, when the 'phone bell rang and Bannerhead Bruce announced the latest sardonic challenge of "Constant Reader ". 

Benskin pursed his thin lips. "Now don't you worry about this, Mr. Bontemps," he remarked. "We've got our plan of action already cut and dried. You and I, accompanied by three of my best operatives, will arrive at the theatre exactly at half-past seven. 

We will go straight to Madame Fotheringay's dressing-room.

I have already fixed that with the stage manager, and I have reason to believe that quite a number of Scotland Yard men will be present among the audience. 

You tell me that Mrs. Fotheringay's turn lasts about ten minutes and is mostly what we used to call ' tableaux vivant'. That is, she is not acting much, but takes the centre of the stage dressed in very little but tulle?" 

"Zat is quite right, Inspector," agreed M. 

Bontemps. "I haf my car outside and also I haf dis -and a licence for it," he added, patting his hip pocket suggestively. 

Benskin gave a sour smile. Like most of the 

C.I.D., he did not approve of firearms.

Meanwhile, Bannerhead Bruce had been busy.

The audacious 'phone call he had received from the mysterious correspondent had finally convinced him that a big story was about to break. He had conveyed the information to a dubious MacWhiffle, and by methods in which he was an expert had secured permission to stand in the wings during Mrs. Fotheringay's performance. 

Bannerhead Bruce, however, was not the only person who was interested in the public appearance of 

La Belle Fotheringay in the fifty-thousand-pound tiara and little else.

Had the whole affair been a vast publicity stunt it could not have attracted a better house. For hours a patient queue of those strange middle-aged women who seem to have nothing to do except wait in interminable queues, knitting with all the calm detachment of widows at the foot of the guillotine, had emerged from whatever haunt they exist in, when there is no first night. 

Bill Kellaway, accompanied by the dutiful Henry, had secured a box for himself. Bill, being an old newspaperman, realised that a great national daily like the "Ray " would not fall for a mere publicity scheme, and certain things that he had heard at the "Q" club had kindled his combative and detective instincts.

Henry's keen eye had already noticed the black, unobtrusive Flying Squad tender, which was drawn up beside the stage door, and recognised his old enemy, Inspector Barker of the C.I.D.

He mentioned the fact to Bill, who grinned appreciatively. It was like old times. Barker was no fool, and unless there was something in the rumour besides publicity, it was hardly likely that the Yard would send a Flying Squad tender to the Frivolity.

"Are you sure it was Barker, Henry?" he inquired. 

The valet smiled. 

Yes, sir," he rejoined. "It is some considerable time since I have seen the worthy man, but Inspector Barker, if I may say so, is the kind of individual who impinges himself on one's memory. He is unique in one sense at least. He is the only man at Police H.Q. who remotely resembles the popular conception of a Scotland Yard detective as delineated by the writers of detective stories." 

Bill chuckled. He knew Inspector Barker of old.

Most crooks, to their cost, made the mistake of treating the Inspector like a fool, and in fairness to Barker, it must be stated that he did everything to live up to his fictitious reputation. 

His square-toed boots, his waxed moustache and aggressive bowler hat were, in reality, a subtle form of disguise. He deliberately pretended to look a bigger fool than he was, but his red face and somewhat cod-like eyes masked a shrewd and efficient brain. 

In reality Barker was one of the cleverest C.I.D. men at Police H.Q.

His hobby was paleontology. He spoke three languages fluently, and if he was a little weak on spelling, he had a sound knowledge of the classics. 

Bill Kellaway, therefore, was expecting something rather out of the ordinary. Henry hovered discreetly in the background of the box, and Bill listened with ill-concealed impatience to the overture. 

The first turn, that of Japanese acrobats, he gazed at with mild condescension. He was rather too sophisticated to enjoy the hackneyed jokes of the 

Lancashire comedian with the red nose and the grotesque suit; but the massed audience of the

Frivolity seemed to be in high good humour, and loudly applauded the man's vulgar witticisms. 

In her dressing-room La Belle Fotheringay rubbed her rounded arms and shoulders with wet white, heavily rouged her cheeks, and daubed Mascara on her eye-lids. At close quarters the effect was rather startling, but heavy make-up was necessary on the vast revolving stage of the Frivolity.

Two dressers hovered about her like anxious attendant acolytes. She was dressed in a shimmering, diaphanous garment of tulle glittering with sequins with breast plates of shining silver. Her mouth was a scarlet and petulant splash as she studied her make-up critically in the mirror.

It was evident that the house had been thrilled by the Ray's announcement of the attempt to be made on the Antoinette tiara. 66

From his vantage point in his box, Bill Kellaway could see half a dozen burly plainclothes men in the wings, and he recognised several familiar faces in the front row of the stalls.

A queer excitement gripped him. He sensed that if the robbery was to be committed at all, it would be committed in a spectacular and sensational fashion. 

La Belle Fotheringay, conscious of the effect she was making, stood for a moment, hands on hips, while the spotlights picked out a myriad coruscating points of fire from her tiara. 

Then, suddenly, it happened.

CRACK!

Frivolity and in that instant every light went out.

CRACK! CRACK! 

Again, above the hoarse murmur of the audience, the reports rang out, and even as the echoes of the last reverberated in the dome of the theatre, the lights flashed on again, revealing a scene of indescribable confusion.

The sudden transition from darkness into dazzling light half blinded Bill for a moment. When he became accustomed to the glare he saw the limp, motionless figure of La Belle Fotheringay lying face downward on the boards in the centre of the stage. 

There was no sign of her diamond tiara, and his keen eyes noticed that the pearl necklace no longer encircled her slender throat.

A gasp of astonishment, followed by a solemn murmur of rage like the breakers of an angry sea, swept over the audience.

Somebody yelled: "Stop thief!" and then, with a swish, the two-thousand-pound brocaded curtain of the Frivolity swept downward.

A diminutive figure appeared before the footlights and held up his hands for silence, but the audience refused to be baulked of its excitement, to which it gave full throat, like hounds at bay. 66 "Ladies and gentlemen," shouted the manager, and Bill could see the sweat pouring down his face as he agitatedly waved his arm for silence. There is no necessity for alarm. Kindly resume your seats.

The theatre is surrounded by detectives" Whether the last word had a sobering effect on the audience, or whether they felt that they were participating even in a humble fashion in a thrilling crook drama, Bill could not decide, but it was significant that they settled down almost immediately, almost licking anticipatory chops.

Most of them undoubtedly thought that the whole thing was a carefully engineered stunt, and many exchanged sheepish glances. Bill was not to be deceived, however. There was no doubt about it in his own mind. The Fotheringay woman was either dead or severely injured. 

Some order was being restored out of chaos.

The manager hurriedly explained that owing to an unfortunate accident La Belle Fotheringay could not continue her sensational act. "Wot abaht them jools?" howled a voice from the gallery. "Please keep your seats," begged the frenzied manager, as the gods began to grow restive and stamp their feet.

CHAPTER XVI CONCERNING THE OLD MAN WHO LIKED SHRIMPS. 

PROFESSOR HUDSON QUIN looked up from the micro scope in the small but well-equipped laboratory which

comprised a portion of the premises of the "Q" club.

Quin frowned a trifle irritably.

A slight smile played round the Professor's thin lips. He replaced the test tube in a rack, turned out the Bunsen burners, and a few moments later entered the luxurious library of the "Q" club.

The group round the fire were Baron Hovenden, the Press magnate, Nigel Cavendish, and Bill Kellaway.

Rupert Gill was busy at the sideboard mixing drinks. Quin swept the group with his inhumanly cold eyes and bowed. Actress's jewels have been stolen before." "As any good Press agent will tell you," broke in Kellaway.

Cavendish yawned. "I know, I know. I claim no merit for the theft, but the way it was accomplished is, I think, unique in the annals of crime. After all, any fool can steal jewels. It takes a touch of genius, however, to steal the jewels in full view of twenty-five hundred people, and surrounded by the cream of Scotland Yard detectives." 

Quin nodded.

It is certainly spectacular, but as I pointed out before, it isn't the crime that gets the most publicity that is the most successful per se. For instance, the little murder that Siva here reminds me that I committed a few days ago received very little publicity save for that exceedingly amusing article about the 'Chalice of Death' and the fish god Dagon by one of Beelzebub's bright young men."

The Professor paused, and smiled sardonically at his fellow members. Death sometimes occurs within two hours," he added, blandly.

Mr. Bannerhead Bruce was nothing if not a gogetter. He was not content to rest on his laurels in having secured one of the most sensational scoops that Fleet Street had obtained for many a day.

Mrs. Fotheringay, he had made himself almost a nuisance with the stage hands at the Frivolity Theatre.

Inspector Barker was a much-worried man during the week that followed the sensational theft. His life was made one long burden by the frenzied objurgations of M. Bontemps, who continually bombarded him with advice, abuse, and invective by 'phone, in person, and by telegrams. His position was not improved by the officiousness of the melancholy Benskin, who, technically at least, was responsible for the safety of the jewels.

Barker had made a regular cross-examination of every member of the theatre staff who could possibly have had any connection with the crime, but at the end of the week he had to admit himself completely baffled. One afternoon Bill returned from luncheon with an acquaintance of his named Selwayn D'Arcy, an impoverished younger son of the notorious Earl of Grinstead, who by reason of his faculty for inventing choice bits of scandal, had secured a precarious entree into the fringe of society.

Gill. Piecing them together, he was able to form a shrewd estimate and confirm his opinion as to the character of his fellow members at the "Q" club.

One of Blair's favourite dicta was that typescript had even more recognisable characteristics than handwriting. Indeed, he had triumphantly vindicated his theory in the notorious Templeton forgery case, to the astonishment of both judge and jury.

Bruce's keen, analytical brain was working swiftly. 

He had seen more than either of the other two had guessed, but he was far too astute a man to betray the fact. When he spoke it was in a lazy pleasant drawl with a hint of banter in his voice, and those who knew

Bruce best were aware that when he adopted that tone he was infinitely more dangerous than usual. "So you know my friend Bill Kellaway, Symph? 

Great chap, isn't he?" "One o' the best," agreed the little man loyally. "We was in stir together," he added, proudly. "Indeed? I suppose that's where he made his money?" said Bruce, in a bantering tone. "Part of it," agreed Symph-which fact was strictly accurate. "Now see here, Symph." The newspaperman leant forward confidentially. "You've known me some time now, and you know that I never let any of the boys down unless they try any funny business. I want to ask you a few questions. I purposely waited till Barker had gone away. Of course, you needn't answer them unless you like," he added, with a disarming smile. 66 

Sure, I'll answer any question yer like, guv'nor," replied Symph, who was by now quite restored to his usual good humour by the newspaperman's geniality. "Then," snapped Bruce, in a voice which, though low, held a hidden menace. "What have you done with the Bontemps jewellery?” 

He shot an accusing finger at the little convict, and his face went the colour of death. 66 

CHAPTER XVIII IN WHICH PLUTO DOES HIS STUFF. 

BILL KELLAWAY was a comparatively early visitor at the Q" club on the night scheduled for Pluto's explanation of the theft of M. Bontemps' jewels.

Rupert Gill was the only one present, but at that moment Quin, accompanied by Cavendish, entered the long smoke room of the club. Bill 

Kellaway was sensitive to atmosphere, and something about Quin's very presence seemed to radiate an aura of evil. Cavendish, however, appeared to be in good spirits. He had with him a small lizard-skin attachécase, which he tossed down on the green baize table. 

When drinks had been ordered, Quin rose to his feet and coughed in his dry impersonal manner. "Well, gentlemen," he remarked, "I think the time has arrived for our friend, Pluto, to explain in what way he has committed a crime novel enough to be included in our diabolic archives. There have been jewel thefts from time immemorial; they have been the stock-in-trade of sensational novelists for more years than I can remember. If our friend, Cavendish, is to have any chance of winning his stake, I propose to call on him to give his explanations." 

Cavendish yawned and crossed over to the little attaché-case and opened it.

Millionaires though they were, the four men could not repress an involuntary expression of surprise as he opened the bag, to reveal the coruscating diamond tiara that had once encircled the proud head of 

France's tragic queen.

Reposing on a bed of dull blue velvet, the jewels took on an added lustre. Coiled neatly in another recess in a lid of the case was the famous rope of pearls. "Professor," said Cavendish, with a smile. "You are somewhat of an authority on precious stones. Just to satisfy all my diabolic colleagues that there is no deception, I would like you to examine these." "But certainly," said Quin, as they all craned forward over the jewel-case.

At that moment a tiny light above the thick baizecovered portal shone green, and pressing a catch above the mantelpiece, Quin opened the door, which was double barred by an electric time lock. 

Baron Hovenden waddled into the room with a complacent smile and a cigar at an angle of forty-five degrees in the corner of his mouth. He seemed to be very well pleased with himself, but he gave an involuntary gasp as the door clicked softly shut behind him and he saw the flashing diadems beneath the crystal electrolier. "Ah ha," he said, rubbing his pudgy fingers. "Choost in time for the demonstration, I see. You have done well, friend Pluto."

He took a seat by the fireplace as Quin lifted up the gems and examined them minutely through his magnifying glass. Bill Kellaway leant negligently against the polished tallboy at one end of the spacious room which had once been the gambling room of the notorious" Hellfire" club. 66 

Undoubtedly these are the genuine articles," 

Professor Quin announced, replacing the gems in their case beneath the brilliant electrolier over the table. 

A quiet smile of satisfaction played round Sir Nigel

Cavendish's face. He crossed over to the far end of the room towards the sideboard to mix himself a drink. "Well, gentlemen," he began. "I suppose it is only fair to explain exactly the method by which I secured these priceless relics."

Bill Kellaway, from his vantage point near the fireplace, watched the cool, debonair baronet from beneath lowered lids as he lit a cigarette. Was it fancy, or could he detect an undercurrent of nervousness in Cavendish's manner? 66 In performing this crime," continued Sir Nigel,

many factors had to be considered, but fortunately

CRACK! 

He did not finish the sentence, for in that instant a loud report sounded in the room, which was instantly plunged into darkness. 66 

Quick, quick," cried Professor Quin, leaping to his feet.

For a moment or two the shock of being plunged from blazing light into inky blackness had bereft Bill of any coherent thought. Something hissed through the air, and he felt a queer thrill as he recalled 

Madame Fotheringay's remarks about a snake.

Vague fancies about a python uncoiling itself in that loathesome dark flitted through his mind. He felt a slight flick, and the glowing end of his cigarette dropped like a ruby to the floor.

A second later the lights came on, and with a startled cry Rupert Gill pointed to the table with a trembling hand. "Look," he cried, and his voice quavered. "The jewels! They've gone!"

CHAPTER XX OF A TYPEWRITER THAT WENT MAD -AND A VOICE FROM THE GRAVE. 

BANNERHEAD BRUCE never let up on a story. When he had recognised the gaunt man who had come to "The Place" and had seen the swift look of recognition that had been exchanged between Symphony Sam and the electrician of the Frivolity, his active brain immediately began to weave a story out of the incident.

For some time he had been certain that there had been some collusion between the thief who had stolen the jewels and a member of the theatre staff. He had been present when Inspector Barker had examined the whole of the stage hands, but so far as he could gather, each had been able to give a fairly satisfactory account of himself. 

When he had shot out his question at Symphony, the little crook had betrayed the most intense agitation, but cross-examine him though he might, Bruce had been unable to shake him. 10

He resolved, therefore, to play a waiting game, and pretended that the question was only a jest. 

Bannerhead Bruce was very thoughtful, however, when he returned to the imposing offices of the "Ray "building in Fleet Street. The identity of the mysterious Pluto alike dazzled and baffled him. new force had arisen in the underworld. He thought of Symphony Sam's newly-acquired wealth, and recalled some strange stories he had heard of Bill Kellaway's activities twelve months before.

Kellaway was admittedly a friend of Symphony's. 

Symphony knew Simon, the electrician, who was almost certainly an accomplice in the plot; ergo, Kellaway knew something of the plot. 66 Thus ran his thoughts as he ascended the steps of the Ray" building, and seated himself at his littered and untidy desk.

Down below, the rumble of the great rotary machines shook the building like the engines of a ship, printing the country editions of the morrow's Ray". Perspiring reporters passed in and out of the swing doors, while Bruce sat scribbling meaningless little squiggles on his writing pad, evolving theory after theory, only to reject them, to account for the latest development in the mystery. 

Suddenly a grimy-faced office boy appeared. 66 

Mr. MacWhiffle wants to see you, sir," he said, in his piping treble, which jerked Bruce from his reverie.

He yawned prodigiously and let fall his oblong monocĺe. MacWhiffle was at his desk, more gaunt and grimy than ever. 66 

You sent for me?" said Bruce. "Sit ye doon, laddie," returned the news editor. "Ha' ye any more details o' this Pluto affair? "

Bruce hesitated. It was not his habit to divulge all

he knew until he had correlated all the facts into one coherent whole. "Well," he said, cautiously. "I got a line on a new angle to-night which may or may not lead to anything. "Mphm!" snorted MacWhiffle. "The Chief has been speaking to me about you to-day. He thinks ye ought to be takin' a rest. Ye've fallen doon badly on this story, laddie." 

Bruce flushed. "What the hell d'you mean?" he demanded. "Didn't I scoop it?" "I ken," said MacWhiffle, wagging an admonitory finger at him. "But did ye follow it up? Here's over a week gone by and no one thing known about it from the time the jewels were nicked. You're losing your old flair, and ye know what the Chief is—he regards the story as belonging entirely to the 'Ray', seein' that we got Pluto's first intimation." "I know," said Bruce, wearily. "But hell's bells,

I've covered every end there is to cover. work miracles." 

I can't "It's up to a guid reporter to work miracles," responded Mac Whiffle, unfeelingly. "The Chief wants the Ray' to beat Scotland Yard to it, an' he told me to-day that unless ye produced the goods within three days he'll assign young Maynard to it." 

Bannerhead Bruce swore. 66 

Blast him! "

It was galling to his pride that he, a star crime reporter, should be hauled over the coals like the veriest cub. "He was thinking that maybe ye ought to be takin' a holiday," continued MacWhiffle. "An' comin' to look at ye, I think he's about richt." 

He glanced at Bruce's face, which was slightly drawn and haggard after many nights of sleepless-

ness. He had tramped through Deptford and the grimy streets beyond Aldgate for nights on end, ferretting out details of the McGee murder. 

Why don't ye let up a bit, mon?" said

MacWhiffle. An' come back to the case with a fresh brain?" 

"Let up be hanged," snarled the crime reporter. "I've got three days to solve this, have I? Watch my smoke."

Was he really going a bit loopy?

Had all this terrific concentration on crime brought him to the verge of a nervous breakdown? He stared at his machine in a dazed fashion, then staggered out of the room, after crumpling the paper into a ball and flinging it into the waste paper basket. He would get a brandy over at the Press Club, he decided, that would pull him together. 

His face looked pale and distraught and he cannoned into Galloway, one of the re-write men and a particular crony of his.

66 

Wotcher, Bannerhead," said Galloway, cheerfully. "What's the matter, old scout?"

With that he marched his colleague into his own sanctuary. "Either somebody's been monkeying about with that machine, or I've got bats," he said. "Do me a favour, Gall, type a couple of sentences on it."

Wondering, Galloway sat down and rattled off his name and address on the newly inserted sheet.

With a strangled oath he fell on his knees and scrabbled feverishly in the waste paper basket. From it he unearthed a crumpled ball of paper, and opened it with fingers that shook. 

Galloway watched him curiously. 66 "You'd better take it away, old man, "" he remarked. 'You'll be cracking up if you don't take care." 

Bruce's face was ghastly as he stared at the crumpled roll of paper he had just opened out. There, plain enough, was the jargon : "Now is the time for all good men to come to the aid of the party."

He sank down into his chair, and felt his limbs trembling violently.

"I-I guess I'm sick," he announced.

Galloway spoke to him soothingly as one might talk to a fractious child. "I'll ring for a taxi for you, old man.' 

Bruce stared dully before him. Was he really getting hallucinations? He had heard of journalists before who had conked out suddenly like this without warning. Unresistingly he allowed himself to be piloted downstairs into the waiting taxi. He had a flat in Trafalgar Square, and Galloway saw him home. "Better turn in, old scout, "his colleague said, sympathetically. "I've got to get back for the 2 a.m. edition. Drink this," he added, pouring out a generous tot of whisky. 

Bruce's smile was sickly, and the glass rattled against his teeth. 

"I'll give you a ring in the morning and find out how you are. It's probably a touch of 'flu,' said 

Galloway, as he took his leave. 

Left alone in his cosy little bachelor flat, Bannerhead Bruce switched on the gas fire, and huddled himself in his chair, resting his throbbing head in his hands. 

Queer, he did not feel ill, save for the throbbing at his temples. He could have sworn that he had heard 

Haviland's voice on the 'phone, and yet again that the letters he had typed were a meaningless jumble. "I suppose it's brain fag, or something," he muttered.

With an effort he rose to his feet, and crossed over to the gramophone. He was very fond of music, and he felt that one of Mozart's string quartets would soothe his frayed nerves. 

Winding up the machine, he searched until he found the record he wanted, and put in a fresh needle.

46 I am steady enough now," he muttered, adjusting it to the edge of the record. 

Suddenly he started back as if shot, for instead of the mellow music of the master, he heard a highpitched, hysterical voice yelling : "I'll get yer yet, Mr. Blarsted Bruce. I'll get yer eff I waits ten years, s'welp me I will. I'm waitin' for yer, Mr. Busybody Bruce, an' when I come out I'll carve yer ter bits.' 

My God!" gasped Bruce, with a shudder. His face was the colour of death. Normally a strongwilled man, his overwrought nerves almost gave way under the strain. He recognised the voice of Soapy

Simms, the arson king of Pimlico, who had been sentenced to seven years' penal servitude, nine months before, owing mainly to Bruce's evidence. He had been led from the dock, and his last words as he went was the very threat that now rang shrilly and stridently through the quiet flat. "I'll get yer yet," squealed the voice again. It seemed to Bruce's dazed fancy to proceed from the gramophone itself, and his flesh crawled with the horror of it, for he knew that Soapy Simms had died at Bleakmoor prison over a month before! 

With an oath Bruce scrabbled at the sound box.

The needle shrieked protestingly against the surface of the record. He snatched it off, and with a hoarse, unnatural laugh, he held it aloft, then dashed it to fragments against the mantelboard. 

For the first time in his career Bannerhead Bruce felt fear. He had gone mad. Always he had had a horror of insanity. Quivering in every limb, he flung himself on the divan, and buried his head in the pillows. 

A few minutes later his taut nerves relaxed and his brain reasserted itself,

"It's rot," he kept repeating. "All tommy rot. 

Another whisky, and I'll turn in."

He poured himself out nearly half a tumbler full of the neat spirit, and started to undress. 

He crossed over to the dressing table, and glanced at himself in the mirror. His face looked white and unnaturally drawn, and his usually smooth chin was now covered by a stubble of beard.

Suddenly he gave a gasp of horror, and the hair on his scalp tingled. Over his shoulder, faint, shadowy, but malignant, leered the mean, rat-like face of Soapy Simms, his slack mouth convulsed with hatred. 

It was too much for Bruce's overwrought nerves. 

With a strangled moan he collapsed and crumpled forward in a dead faint.

CHAPTER XXI INTRODUCES MR. KYKRIOS PAPADAPOULOS OF STAMBOUL. 

P.C. X437 stalked majestically down Piccadilly as befitted a man in whose blue-clad person was vested the guardianship of one of the most aristocratic beats in London. 

It was a crisp, sunshiny morning, with a fresh breeze blowing across the park. Club servants were already hard at work polishing the steps and the gleaming brassware. The constable acknowledged their greetings with a condescending nod.

At the corner near Berkeley Street he slackened his pace somewhat as he beheld the somewhat unusual sight of two white-coated workmen astride a plank

propped upon two ladders before that old-established firm of high-class tobacconists, Kettering & Co.

said "I don't believe there ain't no sich person, the glum-looking sign painter ruefully, unconsciously quoting the immortal words of Betsy Prigg and surveying that awesome name again.

Whatever else M. Papadapoulos was, he was no

Mrs. 'Arris, for promptly at eight-thirty the doors of the shop were opened by the proprietor himself in person. He was clad in immaculate morning coat and in honour of the occasion sported a white camellia in his braided lapel.

He had a swarthy, olive-hued face, with wispy side whiskers, and a very black spiky moustache. His fingers were liberally be-ringed, and he cocked his sleek head on one side, rather like an inquiring sparrow as he studied the sign painters' handiwork. "Ach, zat is goot, excellent," he said. "Ven you haf finished, mine friends, I vill gif you a cigar, the choicest green laranaga." 

All yer wins yer 'as, cigars or nuts," said the younger painter, facetiously.

M. Papadapoulos surveyed with pride the wares behind the glistening plate-glass windows, and by the time that fashionable Piccadilly was awake and promenading, his name shone out in all its pristine glory, while Alf and Bert, having removed their paraphernalia, smoked their ten-and-sixpenny cigars on a seat in Hyde Park with all the proud independence of the British Working Man. 

Bannerhead Bruce recovered consciousness with a cracking headache and a throat as dry as a limekiln.

His tongue felt like a piece of scorched flannel in his mouth, and a deadly feeling of nausea swept over him as he opened his eyes. For a moment or two he lay quite quiet and comatose, striving to recall what exactly had happened.

More by instinct and routine he groped for the switch which invariably dangled above his head in his bedroom, and cursed feebly because his twitching fingers encountered nothing. It was very dark, so

dark that, strain as he might, he literally could not see his hand before his face. 

Wearily he raised himself on one elbow, and reached out for the luminous watch he always kept on the table beside his bed. His fumblings found nothing. Gradually his scattered wits returned and he began to recall the events of the previous evening. 

He had gone home from the office, he remembered, feeling very queer and ill. Galloway-decent fellow 

Galloway-had brought him home. What time was it now?

Petulantly he flung out a hand, but instead of feeling the hard wall of his room it encountered something soft, padded. Padded!

With a moan he half rose to a sitting posture, and the horror returned overwhelmingly. He realised at once that he was not in bed in his cosy flat. The familiar rumble of 'buses, the clank of milk carts that disturbed his morning slumber, were all absent now. 

The silence was deadly, unnerving, absolute. 

With a hoarse cry he staggered to his feet, and stood swaying drunkenly for a moment, then gingerly he stepped forward, his stockinged feet gliding without a sound over what seemed to be a thick pile carpet, and after a few tottering steps his outstretched hands encountered another wall-padded!

Then, just as the full horror of realisation dawned on him, a dazzling white unshaded electric light flooded the darkness with its brilliance. For a moment or two he was blinded, and then he saw. He was dressed in his own pyjamas, and was standing, swaying dizzily, in a small room utterly devoid of furniture, the four walls of which and including the floor, were heavily padded with buff-coloured felt. 66 

My God!" gasped Bruce, as the awful truth broke over him. "Then I am mad. I'm insane."

He blinked stupidly at the light. His left arm ached intolerably, and he felt a wild impulse to dash himself against those drab walls and scream. Sanity prevailed, however. He drew up the sleeve of his pyjamas, and noticed that his forearm was covered with tiny little puncture marks unmistakably the traces of a hypodermic needle.

He laughed bitterly. So he had been violent, then. 

God in heaven, what time was it? Where was he?

He began to shout, but his voice sounded cracked and sepulchral in that silent tomb of the living dead. 

He noticed that one end of the room had a door, also heavily padded, and he beat his hands unavailingly against its yielding softness. 

Half sobbing with panic now, he flung himself face downwards on the floor, and strove to still the panic that threatened to engulf him. How long he lay there, half comatose, he never knew, but as in a dream he became aware of the door opening slowly and two burly uniformed men, accompanied by a grey-haired man in a spotless white coat, stood on the threshold. 

They gazed down at him with neither anger nor pity in their hard eyes. He was only a "case", but they watched him as warily as a man might watch a wild beast.

With a shudder, the journalist noticed that one of the uniformed men carried a strap with heavy brass thongs. 66 

Where, where am I?" he began, in a quavering voice. 66 Well," said the man in the white coat, in a coldly impersonal voice, "how do you feel now?" 

Bruce gave a cracked laugh. "Feel?" he said. "I don't know how I feel. 

Why d'you look at me like that?" he added, in a voice that was half a scream.

He saw a significant glance pass between the two

attendants, and the man in the white coat surveyed him intently. "Now do you understand that you've got to behave yourself, Swindon ?" ..My 

Swindon?" echoed Bruce, mechanically. name's not Swindon, it's Bruce, Bannerhead Bruce." 

Again he noticed a swift, significant glance pass between the three men, and a wave of helpless terror thrilled through the horrified journalist. These men obviously thought him mad; the man in the white. coat was evidently a doctor.

With a supreme effort of will he managed to get control of himself. "See here, doctor," he said, earnestly. “I—I don't want to argue, but I'm quite sane-now. If you will ring up City 002920 and ask for Mr. Mac- 

Whiffle, he will confirm the fact that I am Bruce, a reporter on their staff."

He passed a hand dazedly across his forehead. "How long have I been here, and where am I?" he repeated. 

Come, come, now, Swindon," soothed the doctor. "You have been very ill. You have given us all a great deal of trouble. If you will promise to do as you are told and not persist in these senseless hallucinations that you are somebody else, you will be able to have a nice little breakfast and go back to your nice clean room. 

Bruce stared in horror at the medico. They were humouring him. 

"For God's sake, believe me, doctor," he said, hoarsely. I'm sane, sane as you are. See, I am in full possession of my faculties. Nine fives are fortyfive, nine sixes are fifty-four, nine sevens are sixtythree. The capital of Czechoslovakia is Prague. 

Would you like me to repeat the alphabet backwards?

---

The doctor surveyed him with a cold and speculative glance that chilled his blood. Even to his own ears the whole thing sounded nonsensical, thought 

Bruce bitterly. Besides, did not all madmen consider themselves sane? "Yes, yes, Swindon," said the doctor, soothingly. 

Something snapped in Bruce. The last remnants of self-control gave way. 

Damn you!" he cried. "I'm not Swindon.

Swindon is a town in the Midlands, famous for its railway workshops. My name is Bruce, Bruce, I tell you.' 

With a sudden leap he launched himself full at that mocking white-coated figure, but his feeble strength was no match for the two burly attendants, who hurled themselves on him and flung him down on the padded floor as if he had been a child. 

He lashed out frenziedly with his fists, and his eyes blazed murderously as one burly attendant planted a knee in his chest and held him down. "I tell you I'm not mad," he shrilled. "Be quiet, loony, can't yer," snarled the man. Crack! 

His open hand caught Bruce a stinging slap across the cheek. The newspaperman's eyes flared hate at the cowardly attendant. "Stop, damn you, stop!" "Give him the jacket," snapped the man in the white coat. "You wait-I-I'll murder you," gasped the frenzied but helpless reporter.

Struggle was unavailing, however. With brutal strength his legs were pinioned and he felt himself forced into a hideous canvas jacket with long, grotesque sleeves to which were attached stout leather thongs. 

That'll larn yer, loony," snarled his brutal-faced

guardian, tugging at the thongs until Bruce felt that his chest would burst.

Struggle was useless, and though his eyes still blazed defiance, Bruce realised he was beaten. There was no escape from the dreaded straight jacket. His face was a pitiable sight by the time the attendants had finished with him. Blood oozed from a cut on his lip, his nose was swollen from the savage violence of the blows he had received, and his head rang dizzily.

He gritted his teeth, and his eyes were still defiant. "Somebody'll suffer for this," he hissed. "he "By heck, I'm Bruce of the 'Daily Ray' and when I get out, then- 66 

Quiet, you," snapped one of the men. "Close yer trap. D'yer want to be gagged as well? ’

With a convulsive shudder Bruce relapsed into silence. 66 Quit all this fool talk abaht being a reporter, Swindon," said the other guard. "Wot's the use of strugglin'? Why can't yer be good an' do as yer told?

Bruce said nothing. The full realisation of his terrible plight swept over him. He was imprisoned in a straight jacket in the padded cell of a mad-house -and he was sane-sane, he insisted. 

The door closed silently, and the lights went out, plunging him into the black darkness of despair. 

CHAPTER XXII MA SHAPIRO GIVES ADVICE. 

BILL KELLAWAY attended the next meeting of the 

"Sons of Satan" in a very different frame of 11

mind from the previous occasions when he had entered the "Q" club.

Before, he had attended more out of curiosity and boredom than with any definite plan of campaign, but now that he knew the real purpose of the club, it was the keen-witted Kellaway who had, unaided, won a million pounds as "Hercules" that embarked on the job. This time he realised he was fighting not for himself but in the interests of the community. He and Henry between them had already worked out their counter attack.

It was now a question, as Bill remarked, of going over the top and into action. 

Quin, quizzical as ever, noticed the change in

Kellaway's manner, and noticing, the vague doubts as to Bill's probity became certainties. He dissembled his doubts, however, and his greeting to Bill was cordial enough. 66 

'I've had an elated 'phone call from Hovenden,' he announced. "He seems under the impression that he has succeeded in pulling off a crime which is as near perfect as fallible human nature will allow. That, of course, remains to be seen, he added. “Inci- dentally, Kellaway, have you yourself worked out the details of your own coup?" "You bet your life I have," laughed Bill.

Three days had elapsed since Bill had started his underground offensive against the four millionaires. 

He, however, had reckoned without the protean qualities of Professor Hudson Quin. He had made the mistake of under-rating his opponent. He did not know that Quin had already suspected him of double crossing, and that his life was in danger. Quin was not the sort of man, however, to act precipitately. 

There was a streak of feline cruelty in the scientist's

nature. He wanted Bill to be lulled into a sense of false security, to play with him like a cat with a mouse.

But Quin, in turn, had fallen into a similar error. 

The situation was not without piquancy. He visualised Bill as a well-meaning blunderer with a conscience. Had he known more about that dynamic young man, and also of the Machiavellian mind of Henry Henry, Quin would not have been quite as complacent.

It was nearly ten o'clock before Baron Hovenden came in, accompanied by Sir Nigel Cavendish.

Rupert Gill greeted them boisterously, and Bill Kellaway noticed that the newspaper magnate looked a trifle flushed with an unwonted brilliance in his dark eyes. 66 'Well, my friends," he said, in his thick, guttural voice, "you behold in me the president of the 

Menticide' club." 

Quin glanced at him sharply. 

And what, may I ask, is this latest addition to journalese?" 

Hovenden extracted a cigar from his gold case and inserted it between his thick, fleshy lips. 66 

Simply that I have driven a perfectly sane man mad," he remarked. "And you call it original," laughed Nigel Cavendish, unpleasantly. 

Hovenden frowned. 

My dear fellow, that remark is neither humorous, entertaining, nor instructive. I maintain that a man who simply by power of suggestion succeeds in upsetting the mental balance of one of the cleverest reporters in Fleet Street has achieved something unique in the annals of crime." "Hell's bells," ejaculated Gill. "What are you handing to us?"

Hovenden laughed oilily. "My dear Cavendish, your crime was spectacular enough, I admit, but as Professor Quin will tell you, its very flamboyance almost invited disaster. I happen to know that Bannerhead Bruce, my crime reporter, was getting a lead on it and he had sufficient brains, I fancy, to deduce how the robbery was committed." 

Again he laughed. "He has not those brains now! To-morrow I will show you a rather interesting phenomenon in a private nursing home in Hampstead, a hard-bitten crime reporter babbling of green fields and writing poetry which is meaningless gibberish." 

A slow horror dawned on Bill Kellaway as he lis tened. He understood now the reason for Bannerhead Bruce's unaccountable absence. If Hovenden was speaking the truth, and there was no reason to doubt it, then the most diabolical crime yet planned by the "Homicide" club had been achieved. 

Bill managed to force a laugh, however. "You have yet to prove that he was deliberately driven mad, my dear Hovenden," dear Hovenden," he remarked. "As Dryden said: 'Great wits are sure to madness allied'. I presume you are alluding to Bannerhead

"So be it, gentlemen, the call has come somewhat sooner than I expected, but I am now in a position to accomplish the most unique and spectacular murder in history.
