CHAPTER XXIII THE HORROR OF HAMPSTEAD HEATH. 

BANNERHEAD BRUCE awoke with a long-drawn-out eldritch scream ringing in his ears. Beads of perspiration dewed his forehead, and he clutched con-

vulsively at the bedclothes. He had been seven days in that house of horror, watched, watched incessantly by the cold implacable eyes of his gaoler. Was he mad or sane? He did not know.

He realised now the futility of arguing. They said he was Swindon-perhaps they were right. In any case it was better to acquiesce and agree to each one of his captor's suggestions however fantastic they sounded than to endure the silence and the terror and loneliness of that padded room. 

He awoke shuddering now as the scream died away, and silence, awesome and terrible, brooded over Heathfield.

He had been transferred that morning from the padded cell to a ward on the second floor of the pri vate nursing establishment. It was a long, bare room, with white enamelled walls, a truckle bed smelling faintly of drugs. The windows were heavily barred, and he knew that an attendant was always on duty near by. He had seen the man's cold, impersonal eye peep in through the Judas hole in the stout wooden door which was barred from the outside and not the inside. 

Bruce, hero of a hundred fantastic adventures in search of newspaper copy, had never felt quite so helpless and impotent as he did at this moment. His mind was numbed, his intelligence dimmed and dazed by the trials he had undergone. He did not realise until long afterwards how his brain had been atrophied by the injection of hyoscin and that he was the victim of one of the foulest and most despicable plots ever evolved by an unscrupulous employer and a sadistic doctor. 

He raised himself on one elbow, his heart thumping irregularly.

CRACK! 

Something crashed through the window, and the

night once more became hideous with clamour. Again that scream rang out, hideous, long-drawn, and horrible. Something fell to the floor with a thud, and the tinkle of broken glass roused his dazed senses into wakefulness. 

Trembling and uncertain, the newspaperman scrambled out of bed. The moonlight streamed through the window, and the fresh night breeze blew in through the jagged hole between the bars. In its silver radiance Bruce saw a heavy stone which had obviously been taken from the rockery at the end of the garden. Attached to the stone by a thin elastic band was an envelope addressed to himself. 

With shaking fingers he took up the missive, and tore it open. 

At that moment he heard the hoarse voice of 

Sweeny, the attendant, bellowing lustily. "By Cripes, the monster's loose. Quick! Wake the docther."

Shivering uncontrollably, Bruce opened the contents of the envelope, and thrust it into the pocket of his pyjamas.

BANG! 

A revolver shot cracked through the night's silence, and the hideous scream was repeated again, dying down to an agonised moan. Somebody pounded on the door. He heard the flap of the Judas hole being lifted, and a hoarse voice inquired: "You all right, Swindon?

He strove to still the beating of his heart, and lay flat on the bed. Again the knock was repeated, and with a terrific effort of will he gave a creditable imitation of a snore. He heard a muttered imprecation, and the sound of retreating footsteps. A wild elation surged through his brain. Did this mean rescue, or- 

Another shot rang out from the moonlit garden,

and the newspaperman in Bannerhead Bruce rose supreme. Escape was impossible, he knew that. The bars outside his windows were of chilled steel half an inch in diameter and four inches apart. The corridor, he knew, was guarded night and day, and yet -and yet he touched the pocket of his pyjamas and felt the comforting crackle of the note he had received. He must have got some good friends outside otherwise what was the meaning of this midnight hullaballoo?

Meanwhile, down in the unkempt garden of Heathfield, Symphony Sam Hicks, having negotiated the perilous spikes on the wall, had looked around for a preliminary reconnaissance. He had just landed pussy-footed on the soft grass when something lumbered towards him from a dark clump of rhododendron bushes near the house itself.

At first Symphony had stiffened into immobility, thinking that the object that came lolloping across the grass was a dog. And then he saw-and the hair on his scalp tingled with terror.

Advancing grotesquely over the long grass was the ghastly figure of an ape dressed in queer, fantastic garments. The moon, which had hitherto been hidden by a mass of clouds, now shone forth, and 

Symphony saw, and his flesh crawled.

What he had taken to be a grotesque ape was obviously a stunted dwarf about four feet in height. 

Its long arms reached to its knees, two yellow fangs protruded from its top jaw, and it capered and laughed insanely. "Ohe-ohe-ohe, what fun! I am the man that proves that Darwin was wrong.

It capered round a clump of chrysanthemums and its shrill eldritch laughter made the little burglar's blood run cold. 66 "Aha!" yelled the monster. Here we have

homo sapiens. They cannot touch me. They say I am mad, but I'm not. An ape cannot be responsible, and whoever heard of a mad ape, anyway? He, he, he!"

"Ho, ho, ho!" howled the monster, and Sam shuddered, for he saw that the creature's arms as he stretched them towards the moon, were covered by long, coarse hair. "Look at me, the missing link, and no one can hold me responsible. Drink, pretty creature, drink," it howled, in a shrill, high-pitched voice. "I'll dig my fangs into your throat, Mr. Man.

BANG!

Cassidy, grab hold of that brute; otherwise-

that Bill Kellaway's voice interspersed through the babel of sound. His one ambition was to escape from this ghastly house and grounds that were haunted with gibbering monstrosities.

CHAPTER XXIV BILL DIGS A PIT. 

"SEVEN O'clock, sir, and at the moment all is well." 

Henry, entering Bill's bedroom with a silver tray,

on which reposed a steaming cup of tea and a folded copy of the "Daily Ray ", drew back the curtain.

Bill sat up in bed with a jerk. He took one look at the grey fog outside and shivered. 

Bill was feeling a little liverish after his night's adventures. He had dropped Symphony close to Ma 

Shapiro's, and reached his home by a roundabout route, to find the faithful Henry waiting for him. 

Save for the sudden apparition of the hideous dwarf in the grounds of Heathfield, Bill's project had gone off according to plan. He judged rightly that the monstrosity was one of Doctor Saltaire's patients who had escaped the observation of his guards. 

Luck had certainly been with both Bill and Symphony, but he wondered uneasily what the day would bring forth.

Henry, his invaluable ally, had made discreet inquiries through a butler friend of his at Hampstead, and Bill was beginning to have a profound respect for his valet's secret intelligence system. 

Bill put down his teacup and yawned. "Seven o'clock, you say? Gosh, Henry, we'll have to hustle. I think I've put checkmate to that blighter Hovenden at least, but it's Quin I'm afraid of. Heaven above knows what devilry he's planning.' Bill pulled thoughtfully at his morning cigarette. "Let's have a look at Slade's report," he said. 

Bill, in his campaign against the "Q" club, had, with Henry's assistance, organised a counter espionage system.

Stanton Slade was the head of a well-known private detective agency, which rejoiced in their nickname of Slade's Sleepless Sleuths". Slade was a relative of Henry's, and Bill, by the simple expedient of paying a vast retaining fee, had secured the agency's services entirely on his own behalf.

He glanced now at the typewritten report which chronicled, hour by hour, the movements of the "Q" club members. A slow smile played round the corners of his lips. 

Your cousin seems to be a very efficient man, Henry," he remarked. "Slade was always painstaking, sir," said Henry. "Not brilliant, perhaps, but indubitably a plodder. 

Strange, at school he was something of a dullard, and had ambitions to enter the Church. I was, fortunately, enabled to dissuade him from that disastrous course, and you may rely entirely on his discretion. 

You will note that Professor Quin's movements are somewhat erratic."

Bill leapt out of bed, his eyes shining with excitement. "And M. Papadapoulos, Henry?" he said. “It looks to me as if—" 66 'Yes, sir," said Henry, and his eyes were full of a subtle and secret mirth. "There is always M. Papadapoulos to reckon with." 

If Bill Kellaway had had an eventful night, the day that followed was, as he expressed it to Henry, even heavier with doings. Kellaway's offensive was on.

Apart from having the movements of each of his fellow members watched by Slade's sleuths, Bill had planned to gain Bannerhead Bruce as an ally.

The latter was a problematical venture, however. If 

Hovenden was correct, Bruce by this time would have been a drivelling idiot in the house of Doctor Saltaire at Hampstead. Bill, being an old journalist however, knew something of the newspaperman's chi canery and resilience. Hence his dramatic coup of the previous night. 

He trusted Bannerhead Bruce, for he knew that 12

there was precious little love lost between the reporter and Baron Hovenden. As Bill explained to Henry, the great difficulty in his campaign against the Homicide Club was the fact that, ostensibly at least, he was supposed to acquiesce in all their crimes. 

Bill exulted in the thought, however, and he had several cards up his sleeve which even the astute brain of Professor Hudson Quin had not suspected. 

Towards afternoon the smoke fog which had enveloped London in its murky embrace had lifted a little. Dropping in at the "Q" club after a strenuous morning, Bill found Baron Hovenden elatedly discussing what he described as his menticide. "I haff choost had a 'phone call from my goot friend Saltaire," announced the newspaper magnate. "It seems there was a burglary in his highly-respectable nursing home last night." "Indeed?" said Bill, with well-simulated surprise. "A lunatic asylum seems to me to be more Îike a place to break out of than into.” 

Hovenden nodded, and played a tattoo with his podgy fingers on the arm of his chair. "This man Saltaire is an excellent fellow," he remarked. "The secrets of his house in Hampstead, could they be told " He made an expressive gesture. "You know little, my dear Kellaway, how many secrets of society are hidden behind the discreet walls of private nursing homes." 

But," said Bill, "I understood that it required the signature of two doctors to certify a man as being insane? "But naturally," said Hovenden, with a chuckle, 66 'Doctor Saltaire, and his colleague, Doctor Schwartz, are fully qualified alienists with most accommodating consciences. Poor Bruce," he added, and Bill could have kicked him for his sneering

smile. "Such a clever young man, but unfortunately suffering from the taint of madness. He has been working hard lately, far too hard, and a little sojourn at Heathfield will doubtless nurse him back to health and sanity again." "Serve him right, the beast," broke in the voice of the Hon. Rupert Gill. "I've met that fellow, Bruce, a nasty, low-down reporter. Did you say it was a nervous breakdown, Hovenden?" 

The press magnate winked. "This afternoon you will see for yourselves," he remarked, turning to Sir Nigel Cavendish. "It suits me," said that worthy. "Though I have heard there are pleasanter ways of spending an after. noon than in a private loony house." 

It was at that moment that Professor Hudson Quin entered from his laboratory. His thin face and greygreen eyes looked more hawk-like and vindictive than ever. 66 'Well, gentlemen," he announced. "It does not give me any particular pleasure to enter a nursing home and watch the insane, or listen to their futilities.

On the other hand, it is my duty as president of the club to adjudge fairly whether Baron Hovenden has succeeded or otherwise in justifying his assertion. 

The modus operandi we will, of course, leave until later, when we come to our final decisions as to who merits scooping in the pool. "I cannot afford to waste much time, for as you know, I must make good my assertion that I shall commit the most unique murder in history and get away with it."

Again his cold eyes rested searchingly on Bill Kellaway's clean-cut face, and Bill felt that the man was trying to dominate him. He had an uneasy sense that Quin suspected him, and knew that if that were

really the case his own life would not be worth a moment's purchase. 

"Well, gentlemen," said Hovenden, with his oily chuckle. You're quite at liberty to bring along with you any discreet doctor, and I am certain he will vouch for the fact that Bannerhead Bruce is non compos mentis. I have made an appointment with my good friend, Dr. Saltaire, for four o'clock this afternoon." 'A visit to a refined modern Bedlam would not be uninteresting," laughed Sir Nigel Cavendish. "I am creditably informed," said Hovenden, "that his mania is quite harmless. He spends most of his time scribbling perfectly meaningless pieces of poetry. He has paranoia, of course, and suffers from the delusion that he is the managing director of the 

Great Midshire Railway." 66 

What?" ejaculated Bill Kellaway, in surprise. "That's a queer form of mania to acquire? 

Hovenden chuckled. 

"If you'd seen the clever way the whole thing was suggested to him, you would realise its subtlety. He has been drugged for a week with hyoscin, and a course of suggestion allied with mesmerism practised upon him. He is always referred to there by the name of Swindon. Somehow or other his crazy mind connects Swindon with the manufacture of railway trains. At first, when his mania was at its height, he imagined himself to be a train, but now he manages to convince himself that he is the greatest railway director on earth. "I was amused this morning when Saltaire reported that he has been clamouring for a Bradshaw, as he wishes to set it to music." 

Bill Kellaway chuckled, but his mirth was not the callous mirth that the others imagined it to be.

"How, exactly, did you achieve your menticide, Hovenden?" he inquired. "You had better wait, my impetuous young friend," interrupted Quin. "There's plenty of time to explain when we are entirely convinced of the man 

Bruce's lunacy." "He's mad all right," announced Hovenden, confidently. "I've ordered the car for three-thirty. I presume you will all accompany me, gentlemen?" 

Bill Kellaway nodded. Things were working according to plan. He touched his hip pocket, and felt the cold comforting feel of his automatic. The gloves were now off. He was straining every nerve and sinew not only to circumvent the machinations of the "Q" club, but to dig a pit into which they would fall unsuspecting his complicity until the showdown came.

He was glad to learn that Hovenden treated the burglary at Heathfield as a minor affair. Little did the newspaper magnate realise that Bill had deliberately planned the raid in order to get in touch with

Bannerhead Bruce. Whether the journalist had sufficient strength of will or sanity left Bill had no idea, but he was taking a chance at long odds, and in the meantime, as Henry remarked, there was always the question of M. Papadapoulos. 66 

CHAPTER XXV AN AFTERNOON IN BEDLAM. 

You will have visitors to see you this afternoon, Swindon," announced Dr. Saltaire to the thin, hag-

gard-faced young man with the wispy beard who sat with a pencil and writing pad on his knee in the cool white ward of Heathfield nursing home. 

Bannerhead Bruce looked up listlessly. His eyes were glazed and dull, and he drooled a little at the corner of his mouth. Saltaire, a stocky man with a square head and bristly close-cropped hair, bent forward suddenly and seized his patient by the wrist. "Listen to me, Swindon; you will behave yourself," he hissed. "Remember you have been very ill, and if you make a fool of yourself, or me, thenhow would you like another taste of the jacket, Swindon? 

Bannerhead Bruce shivered as he recalled the agonising torture and degradation he had endured for three days in the padded room in the basement of the building. So he was getting visitors, eh? Gradually his numbed wits had come back to some semblance of sanity.

When the stone had crashed through his window the previous night, Bruce had almost reconciled himself to the fact that he was doomed to be imprisoned as a lunatic for the rest of his natural life. But for the crackling note which he had hidden beneath his mattress, the newspaperman would have given up all hope. As it was his pulses leapt at the thought that there was a chance of escape if only he had the nerve to bring it off.

The instructions in the note had been explicit. 

Mentally he prayed that his almost shattered nerve would not give out. He relaxed himself wearily in his wheeled chair. He knew that if he stayed in this diabolical place much longer the last thread of sanity would snap irrevocably. 

He was conscious conscious of the doctor's speculative hypnotic glare, and began to scribble foolishly on his pad-silly meaningless rhymes:

"Romance brings up the nine-fifteen 

That sailed the wintry sea 

And she had a little porringer that sang 

God save the queen. 

He never smiled again 

Though all but he had fled 

And if you're shooting Dan McGrew 

My love he'll be a long time dead."

With trembling hands he tore off a leaf and proffered it to the doctor. 66 

Puff, puff, choo, choo, I'm the only railway engine that can write poetry," he said, in a thick voice. I used to be a criminal once-murdered millions-I dismembered Crippen-poisoned Patrick 

Mahon-dear good fellow Patrick," he said, with a foolish giggle. 

"Stop that," snapped Dr. Saltaire. "Go and sit down; otherwise- He broke off significantly as the door opened, and the burly Irish attendant came into the room. "If you please, sorr, the gentlemen have arrived,' he announced. 

Bannerhead Bruce bit back the exultant shout that rose to his lips. Dazed as he was, he realised that

Bill Kellaway had kept his word. It was up to him now.

It needed a tremendous effort of will to deceive such an astute psychologist as the Herr Doktor 

Saltaire, but Bruce, up to now, had succeeded. He even found a certain mental stimulus in the absurdrhymes and meaningless jingles he had written. But for the after effect of hyoscin and the brutal treatment he had received in the straight jacket, Bruce would have invented a plan of escape himself, but his nerves were very near to breaking point.

Dr. Saltaire shot him a warning glance, and

Bannerhead ceased his absurd gyrations round the room. Seizing his jotting pad he began to scribble again furiously. "You had better show the gentlemen up," snapped the alienist. 

Bruce's heart pounded violently against his ribs.

He would have to seize his opportunity under the lynx eye of the physician. Bill Kellaway's note had contained explicit instructions, and Bannerhead Bruce was keyed up to the very last ounce of his physical and mental strength.

The door opened, and Baron Hovenden stood on the threshold. The surprise to Bruce was staggering, but by a tremendous effort of will he managed to dissemble. The magnate was followed in turn by 

Quin, Bill Kellaway, Cavendish and the rest of the 

Q" club members. 

Saltaire shot a warning glance at Hovenden, and turned with a forced smile to Bruce. "Now, Swindon, here are some friends of yours,' he remarked. "Surely you recognise them?" 

Bannerhead Bruce at that moment put over the best piece of acting he had ever put over in his life. 

He looked up with his glazed and fishy eyes and grinned idiotically at Hovenden. "Friends, Romans, Countrymen," he said, with a stupid giggle. "I'm the Laureate of the Line. The 

Locomotive Laureate with steam in my veins."

He got up shakily from his wheeled chair and bowed. "I know you," he said, wagging a playful forefinger at the newspaper proprietor. "Your name is 

Yeastvite. You're worth a guinea a box. But what you want is plenty of vitamins, and bubbling sizzling steam in your veins like I've got." 

He laughed foolishly.

"What is your name, my man?" demanded Professor Quin, and his agate-hard eyes seemed to bore through Bannerhead Bruce.

With a prodigious effort, the journalist managed to emulate a high-pitched hysterical laughter. He hugged himself delightedly, and then said, in a tone of owlish gravity: "That's just it. Watt is my name. I'm the celebrated inventor of the steam kettle. I'm the only man who can balance the Flying Scotsman on a teaspoon, that's me." "See," he grabbed hold of Bill Kellaway suddenly by the sleeve. "I've written a poem, a million verses long, all about Molochs and monarchs and midnight mails."

He grabbed hold of his jotting pad and as the perspiration poured down his pallid face, began to chant : "Between the dark and the daylight 

When my veins are sizzling with steam

There is some corner in a foreign field 

And things are not what they seem.

She was only an Esquimau's daughter 

In the year of jubilee 

But we shall not lightly sheath the sword 

To be or not to be. "Don't you think that's wonderful?" inquired 

Bruce earnestly of Kellaway. "Undoubtedly a paranoiac," said Professor Quin, studying Bannerhead Bruce intently. "Puff puff, choo choo," said Bannerhead Bruce, flinging his arms up in the air and giving a longdrawn shriek in imitation of a railway train's whistle. 

Then, in a cracked voice, he sang:

"Oh, Bridget dear, now can you hear

The news that's going round It's steam and not velocipedes 

That's all the rage of town. 

There's blood upon the bread me boys 

And Jabberwocks at that 

And out spake brave Horatius 

He was steaming through his hat." 

Baron Hovenden rubbed his thick fleshy fingers together and beamed at the Hon. Rupert Gill. "Did I not tell you, the man is completely mad." "Mad?" snarled Bannerhead Bruce, with a sudden change of front. "What d'you mean? I'm a criminal. I boiled Dr. Crippen alive in steam.

Fifteen men with chests full of steel. Think what they've been, God save the queen." 

Quin glanced significantly at Dr. Saltaire. It seemed obvious that the journalist's dementia was getting more and more pronounced, for Bannerhead 

Bruce capered about like a mad thing, pawing in turn each of his visitors. It was while Quin was exchanging a few whispered words with Dr. Saltaire that Bill Kellaway saw his chance, and took it.

He winked once at his erstwhile Fleet Street colleague, and then with a gesture swift as the dart of a snake he slid a loaded automatic out of his pocket into Bruce's hand. He had no opportunity of saying more than two words, but those two were significant. "Beat it," hissed Bill Kellaway, below his breath. 

Bannerhead Bruce felt a wave of exultation sweep over him. He was clad only in his pyjamas and a dressing jacket of some grey drab material. With a wild cackle of laughter he spun round like a teetotum.

"Behold me, Moloch, the only poet in the world who lives on steam. Hot, scalding steam.' 

For a moment or two Bill was taken aback. If 

Bruce were really acting it was one of the finest exhibitions of histrionics he had yet seen. On the other hand, Bill felt a queer foreboding. What if the man were really mad? 

The next three seconds Bill never rightly recalled, for with a triumphant howl Bannerhead Bruce backed to the doorway, the automatic gripped unwaveringly in his hand. “I'm mad, I'm mad, I'm mad," he yelled. “I'm going to shoot every one of you in the stomach-it pains more there. Put up your hands, and remember you can't hang me because I'm insane and not responsible for my actions." 

Hovenden's face went a dirty putty colour. "Mein Gott, Saltaire, quick. See, the lunatic is armed." "Aye, armed," laughed Bannerhead Bruce, exultingly. "Armed and irresponsible." 

With a lightning movement he stepped back to the door of the ward and shot back the catch. It was bolted as he knew only too well, on the outside, but his brain, ice cool now that he held the whip hand, knew that the bolt would be shot back during his visitors' presence. Whatever his defects, Dr. Saltaire was a brave man. "Put down that toy at once, Swindon," he snapped.

CRACK! 

A tongue of flame spurted out of the squat round muzzle of the automatic, and a bullet smacked into the wall an inch above the surgeon's head. 66 

Through the stomach next time," howled Bruce, triumphantly. His eyes blazed. Gone was the lacklustre appearance and Bill Kellaway thrilled. He

had a wild impulse to range himself beside his old colleague of Fleet Street, but realised how fatal that would be if he was going to smash irrevocably the degenerates of the "Q" club. 

Stop that, you thief," he snapped venomously. 

With an exultant laugh Bannerhead Bruce fired again. The bullet whizzed past Kellaway's ear.

For a moment or two everyone was too dumbfounded by Bruce's sudden volte-face to be able to collect his thoughts. "Yes, shoot you in the guts,' "screamed the journalist. "The man who follows me will get the contents of this magazine in his belly," he added. 

There was a crash as he disappeared through the enamelled steel door, and shot back the bolts from -the outside. His heart was pounding like a sledge hammer, and his breath came in great racking pants. 

"Good old Kellaway," he exulted. According to plan. According to plan." 

He took a flying leap down the stairs, heedless of the clamorous pounding of fists against the locked door of his late prison. He alighted in the hallway with a thud, and found one of the attendants barring the entrance. Jamming his gun into the man's ribs, Bannerhead Bruce threatened to pull the trigger unless the guardian instantly opened the outer door.

Terrified at this sudden new menace to his comparative safety, the warder shot back the hasp and with a triumphant howl Bannerhead Bruce raced across the lawn. He made a wild and fantastic figure in his flapping pyjama suit and woollen bed jacket, but he cared not a jot for appearances. He was free! He was free!

He guessed that the others had arrived in a limousine. Bill Kellaway had hinted that a car would be waiting at the tradesmen's entrance.

Wrenching back the lock, he found himself in the suburban respectability of Heathfield Avenue. 

Drawn up at the kerb was a speedy-looking 

Hispano Suiza car. Bannerhead Bruce was taking no chances. He glanced at the driver, a swarthy man with an aggressively-waxed moustache. "Drive," he ordered curtly, opening the door of the tonneau and hopping in beside the man.

He slammed the muzzle of his automatic hard against the other's ribs. "Drive-like hell-otherwise I'll press this trigger and you'll go there." 66 

Certainly sair," said the driver. "What's your name?" demanded Bruce, who by this time was half crazy with the excitement of the past few minutes. 

The driver smiled as he let in his clutch, and the great car bounded forward like a living thing. 66 My name, sair," he said, with a bland and ineffable smile, "is Kykrios Papadapoulos."

CHAPTER XXVI IN WHICH SYMPHONY NEARLY SIGNS THE PLEDGE. 

THE cold blue flame of the fire in the "Q" club danced eerily and fantastically round the imitation logs. 

There was silence. There was no one, save for a furtive figure that crept noiselessly from the pantry beyond Professor 

Quin's laboratory.

It was five o'clock on the afternoon following 

Bannerhead Bruce's sensational escape from Heathfield House and the "Q" club at that hour was devoid of its Satanic inhabitants. 

Symphony Sam Hicks glanced cautiously about him, and, reassured, tiptoed across the Bokharan rug. 

Symphony Sam's presence in the "Q" club was by design and not accident. Bill Kellaway was responsible for it, and Bill, who had quite enough on his hands, had advised the little burglar to steal back his own finger-prints. 

Symphony's arrival on the doorstep of the "Q" club was singularly undramatic. Through Kellaway's aid he had secured the uniform of a gas inspector. He had knocked very noisily on the door, and even as his left hand played a rat-tat-tat, his right inserted a flexible piece of steel wire into the Yale lock.

Symph was a firm believer in the old adage: "Never let thy right hand know what thy left hand doeth." "And na," said Symphony Sam, drawing on a pair of rubber gloves and glancing round the large room where once the exquisites and bucks of the 

Regency period had strutted, diced, and gambled, "And na, wot abaht it?" 

He groped cautiously with his rubber-gloved hand at the side of the desk and pressed an ornate piece of inlay.

Bill Kellaway had told him that his rehabilitation would be complete if he pressed a certain rose. He found it, and suddenly a drawer concealed in the top of the desk shot out, almost hitting him in the chin. "Cripes," said Symphony Sam, as his gloved fingers closed over the confession and the damning thumb prints.

Even as he did so a voice spoke out of the drawer. "Eureka!"

It was the voice of M. Papadapoulos. 

Symphony Sam started back as if he had been shot when that fantastic voice hit him almost like a physical blow. 

Holy Cripes!" gasped Symphony Sam. "I've got the willies', that's wot I've got-all of 'em. 

Will, Bill, an' Jill."

He looked furtively over his shoulder. "Symphony," said a voice from the drawer. 

The little crook crossed himself. 

S'welp me bob, I won't drink another drop as long as I live." 

Symphony," repeated the voice.

Trembling violently, Sam crossed over to the sideboard and helped himself to a stiff whisky and soda. 

The glass clattered like a castanet against his teeth. "" Sam.' 

It was the persuasive voice of Henry Henry which spoke, and with a muttered imprecation, the little crook spun round on his heel, expecting to see the stolid-faced manservant of his china Bill Kellaway. 66 

No one was there. 66 "Don't be silly, Sam," said the disembodied voice.

Take out your confession and place one pedal extremity before another in a horizontal position with as much acceleration as possible-otherwise beat it!"

With fingers that trembled, the little crook reached over to the drawer and seized the document which had caused him so many sleepless nights. "And when you're out," continued that fantastic voice," stop and buy a packet of Woodbines at the shop of M. Papadapoulos."

Symphony Sam was not quite certain whether he was mad, drunk, or both. He had the sense to retain

his rubber gloves, and he picked up his confession and thrust it into his pocket. With a crab-like motion he advanced sideways to the door, and it was at that moment that he found himself gazing into the cold, implacable eyes of Professor Hudson Quin. "Cripes!" gasped Symphony. His nerves were raw and ragged. He stood there, swaying almost drunkenly on the threshold, and realised that he had opened the wrong door. 

Instead of the exit he had found the entrance to the library of the "Q" club, which was dominated by a full length pastel portrait of the implacable Professor. "Gawd!" said Symphony Sam. "Wot with drawers that answer you back an' Quins that ain'tno wonder I'm a bloomin' gas inspector.'

He clanged the door to, and furtively crept down the stairs of the quondam "Hellfire" club. He let himself out with considerably more celerity than he had let himself in, and it was at that precise moment that police constable P.C. X437 ambled slowly along. 

Symphony stood on the doorstep trying to look as innocent as any gas inspector can. The constable eyed him coldly and dispassionately, and Symphony's collar wilted. "Evenin'," said the little crook. "Good evening," boomed P.C. X437. 

Suddenly Symph had a brain wave. "Kin yer tell me eff there's a bloke, a tabacconist rahnd 'ere 'ose name sahnds like a soda water syphon ? ” 

The constable stroked his moustache judicially, and a vague memory came back to his mind. "You mean Pappapap--' 

'Pip, pip," said Symphony Sam, and boarded a Westbound bus with a grin and an agile leap.

CHAPTER XXVII BARON HOVENDEN EXPLAINS. 

In the humming news room of the " Daily Ray " Mr. 

MacWhiffle sat disconsolately surveying the flimsies on his desk. He sucked moodily at his pipe and groaned : "What a story-and there's that puir wee laddie, Bannerhead, ill in a nursing home instead of covering it."

Seven days had elapsed since the news editor had last seen Bannerhead Bruce. Baron Hovenden had been appropriately diabolic in his explanation of the newspaperman's absence. His story rang true, backed up as it was by the testimony of Bruce's friend, Galloway. MacWhiffle, dour and efficient Scotsman though he was, held a certain sentimental liking for the dynamic newspaperman, and now that one of the most amazing murder stories had broken he realised that no one could handle it quite as well as Bannerhead.

Meanwhile, that dynamic newspaperman, whose resilient nature had already recovered from his ordeal at the Hampstead nursing home, was now seated in the rear parlour behind the shop of M. Papadapoulos in Piccadilly. The efficient Henry had disguised him simply and effectively with the aid of a dark brown face stain and a wispy black moustache, and installed him as his nephew, a Greek named Socrates Spiro.

Bannerhead was no mean actor himself, and he filled his not very exacting rôle with commendable efficiency. He was seated now at a small steel table on which was placed an electrical instrument and several 13

complicated batteries and wires-a somewhat incongruous combination to find in a cigar shop. 

A pair of ear 'phones was clamped on his head, and he was busily engaged in jotting down in his rapid shorthand certain conversations to which he had been listening for the past half hour. 

A casual observer might have taken M. Spiro for an earnest young student listening in to the broadcasting of one of the B.B.C. educational talks, but the talk to which Bannerhead Bruce had listened in spellbound astonishment, while undoubtedly educative, was infinitely more enthralling than the most thrilling wireless drama ever broadcast from Savoy Hill.

Bill Kellaway had long since realised that money, judiciously expended, could buy anything. He had paid an extortionate sum for the late proprietor's business, and the omniscient Henry had secured the services of his many technical friends in erecting, with all possible speed and secrecy, a super wireless transmission set which enabled him to listen in to a conversation at the "Q" club, which was situated two or three doors away from M. Papadapoulos' cigar emporium.

Bill's visit to Whitehall was to see an old colleague, a barrister attached to the director of public prosecutions in the Home Office. 66 In a guarded fashion he had explained the peculiar circumstances which had brought about his membership of the Q" club. His friend, Hillyard, had been frankly sceptical at first, and unofficially warned 

Bill that he was playing a dangerous game. He agreed, however, to accompany Bill one evening to

M. Papadapoulos' shop, and he listened in to Sir 

Nigel Cavendish's explanation of the Frivolity theft with stupefaction.

Thereafter there was exceeding activity in the office of the public prosecutor. Thoroughly, efficiently, but

without hurry, the legal aspects of the case were examined, and a plan of campaign agreed upon. 

Serenely unconscious in his arrogant superiority to the forces of law and order, Hudson Quin did not suspect that his every movement was watched, and that hidden ears listened in to every scrap of conversation that took place in the precincts of the "Q" club. 

It was shortly after eight o'clock at night when the "Sons of Satan" held their next full session to hear 

Hovenden's report. Bannerhead Bruce, with pencil sharpened, listened in avidly as he took down a verbatim report of the proceedings.

His mouth set in a grim, hard line as he recognised the guttural tones of his employer boasting of the diabolical scheme he had evolved to drive Bruce mad.

He learnt that the whole thing was a carefully put-up job, and realised for the first time how powerful a thing the power of suggestion can be. 

It was Hovenden who had planted in the mind of MacWhiffle by subtle innuendoes that Bannerhead was on the verge of a nervous breakdown. 

He explained how, by a simple ruse, he had got one of his paid agents, not only to mimic the voice of the sub-editor on the 'phone so as to get Bruce out of the room, but also to plant on his desk an exact replica of his typewriter with all the keys so fixed as to type the wrong letters, while ostensibly in the same order. 

Bannerhead Bruce ground his teeth savagely as he realised the diabolical ingenuity of the plot. He recalled a pimply-faced, shifty-eyed individual named Snaith, who had recently been appointed to the process department. He had never liked the fellow. 

Obviously it was Snaith who had rung the changes on his typewriter and rung them back again when he went out to see Galloway. Undoubtedly it was Snaith who had drugged the coffee which had added so alarmingly to his symptoms.

He learnt, too, the devilish thoroughness with which the newspaper magnate had continued his scheme to drive him over the borderline of insanity. 

How, by the skilful use of a chemical well known to conjurors, whose base was nitrate and mercury, he had caused the face of Soapy Simms to be painted in the mirror in Bannerhead's room. Tilted at a certain angle, the face. was invisible. With the light rays striking the looking glass obliquely, the face appeared in all its hideousness.

The same agent, a skilful and unscrupulous exmagician and actor, who had, Bruce recalled, recently been released from gaol after sentence on a charge of fraud, had planted the fake record in Bruce's gramophone cabinet.

It was the diabolical thoroughness of it all that appalled Bannerhead. But for Bill Kellaway, he realised, he would undoubtedly have been driven really insane. Hovenden had overlooked nothing and a great surge of hatred swept over the newspaperman as he listened to the unctuous voice. Suddenly the thin, reedy tone of Professor Hudson Quin broke in on the Baron's discourse. Bruce turned a dial on the set to amplify the voice. "That is excellent, as far as it goes, my dear

Hovenden, but you must admit that in the circumstances the results are incomplete. Bannerhead Bruce has escaped, and unless he is recaptured I am afraid you will forfeit your stake. Furthermore, I do not believe you have succeeded in establishing the fact that Bruce is actually insane. My personal belief is that he was acting, very cleverly. How he obtained the revolver it is up to you to discover, but while that young man remains at large there is grave danger to all of us." 

Bruce heard his employer swear. "Don't you worry about Bruce. I tell you he's

mad. I've got Schwartz and Saltaire in my pay, and they will vouch for it. Who will heed the senseless ravings of a madman? I am employing detectives to comb London for that swine, Bruce, and mark my words, within twenty-four hours he'll be in the padded cell at Hampstead." 

Beneath his tan Bruce's face blanched. He realised that the Baron would be as good as his word, and once again he blessed Kellaway and the omniscient

Henry for the sanctuary they had provided for him. 

It was at that moment that the voice of Kellaway himself came over the ether. It was, Bruce thought, slightly forced. 

The case at the moment then, Professor, being sub judice, I presume we must wait until you have accomplished the crime you have promised to commit? 66 

CHAPTER XXVIII THE PROFESSOR PRONOUNCES SENTENCE. 

BILL KELLAWAY returned to his flat that evening with that strange sense of foreboding in his heart which at its highest is known as " prophecy ", and at its lowest presentiment of impending evil ". He could not quite account for it, but he felt that Professor Quin not only suspected but knew that he was an enemy in their midst, and that being the case, Bill was uncomfortably aware that his life would not be worth a moment's purchase.

He arrived back and found Henry busily transcrib-

66 ing the shorthand notes which Bannerhead Bruce had given him of that evening's conversation at the Q" club. The session that evening had been short, almost perfunctory, Bill noted. He wondered at the time. 

There had been a strange constraint about each of the four members. Even Nigel Cavendish had lost a good deal of his insouciance. The lot this evening had fallen to the Hon. Rupert Gill, and Bill wondered vaguely what that versatile and effeminate man would put over in the way of crime. 

Had Bill been present in a certain quiet hotel just off the Strand at that moment, he would have realised that the menace which he suspected was both real and immediate. In a private room four of the "Sons of 

Satan" were in session. Quin was speaking, and his strange eyes glowed with unholy hatred as he described Kellaway. "We have been double crossed, gentlemen," he announced, with conviction. "Kellaway is no more of an insomniac than I am. He must be eliminated at once if we are to be secure. True, he will find it extraordinarily difficult to attach any guilt upon any of us. Our crimes have been consummated in far too clever a fashion for that. We have left behind us no clue. 

On the other hand, there is a chance that if we continue, with all due respect to you, Gill, being a mere amateur in crime you may make a tiny slip which will bring the whole edifice crashing about our ears. 

"But good heavens, Quin," broke in Nigel Cavendish, "what makes you suspect Kellaway? He's as bloodthirsty as any of us when it comes to talking.' "Yes," said Quin," but when it comes to performing, what then? How did Bannerhead Bruce obtain possession of that revolver? Kellaway gave it to him, of course. I am certain that all our movements are

being watched-not by the police, but by Kellaway's secret agents. I've been conscious of the fact for days." "But hang it," protested Rupert Gill. "He's up to the neck in it himself. According to the law he is accessory both before and after the fact." 

Professor Quin smiled grimly. "Have you heard of that loophole of the law which is grandiloquently known as 'King's Evidence '? "' he asked. 

Yes, but what are we goin' to do?" broke in Hovenden. "We'll lull Kellaway into a 

Kellaway into a false sense of security," said Quin. "At all costs we must not let him know that we suspect him. To use an expressive 

American idiom," he added, " he must be bumped off and the sooner the better. There are a dozen ways, " he continued slowly and an abstracted look crept into his cold eyes. We must not be too precipitate, however, at this juncture. Once Kellaway knows that he is suspected, he will rush matters and apply for police protection. If I recall rightly, it isn't the first time he has hidden himself in gaol from a private vendetta." 

His eyes glowed malevolently and a little hush of horror fell for a moment on the "Sons of Satan".

CHAPTER XXIX A DECLARATION OF WAR. 

MR. TIMOTHY TRING, the landlord of that ancient hostelry, the "Melford Arms", breathed ster-

torously on a pewter tankard and polished it vigorously with his cloth. He was a tubby, apple-cheeked little man clad in a check suit with a vivid red waistcoat. 

A large grey limousine drew up in the sleepy High

The landlord greeted his fair visitor with a polite bow, and opened the door of the snug little coffee room with a flourish. "Have you a room for myself, and can you put up my chauffeur in the servants' quarters?" said the girl.

She seemed to be a remarkably self-possessed young woman, Timothy thought. "I'll see what I can do, ma'am," he said, obsequiously enough however. 

Splendid," remarked the girl, producing a long jade green cigarette holder and a gold cigarette case. "I suppose this is the best inn in the village?" she added, lighting her cigarette from the match which the landlord promptly offered. "Yes, ma'am, it's the best and the oldest," said

A counsel of war was being held at Bill Kellaway's flat. Events, as Bill shrewdly surmised, were rapidly reaching a crisis, and a closer perusal of Henry's notes convinced him that it would be dangerous for him to attend any further sessions of the "Q" club.

CHAPTER XXX

DOROTHY IS TAKEN FOR A RIDE ". 

SIR JOHN JARVIE, the eminent philanthropist—as the newspapers who carried his jam advertisements invariably described him-strode on the terrace of 

Melford Manor and gazed with the pride of possession over the age-old emerald lawn of the famous old house.

He was a rotund little man whose benevolent features had been for years known to the housewives of the world above the guarantee of purity for his jams in a facsimile of his own handwriting.

This morning Sir John felt at peace with the whole world.

He glanced with approval at his possessions and smiled as the trim, tweed-clad figure of his daughter Dorothy emerged from the stable, a couple of black spaniels lolloping joyfully at her heels. 

Dorothy waved to her father, and mounted the steps of the terrace.

Sir John had a rolled-up paper under his arm, and with a smile she extracted it. "Any news, Daddy? I was out long before the morning papers came, "she explained. 

Sir John grunted. Dorothy was the one soft spot in his nature, and he could deny her nothing. He had intended strolling over towards the park and studying the latest city news. In feminine fashion 

Dorothy turned instinctively to the picture page of the "Daily Flash". " she re- Then, with characteristic impetuosity, she tore out the city page and handed it to her fuming parent. "There's your silly old stock market, marked. "I'm going into the library to read this."

Sir John shrugged his shoulders resignedly as she passed, a trim, athletic figure, into the Manor House. 

After lunch Dorothy felt at a somewhat loose end. 

Her father had gone back to London for one of his interminable board meetings. She decided to ring up Bill. To her surprise she could get no reply to her call, and felt vaguely uneasy. It was not like 

Henry to be absent from duty at this hour. She tried to occupy herself with a magazine, but the stories seemed dull and lifeless. 

Petulantly she tossed it aside and was about to ring for tea when Parsons, the butler, entered with a card on a salver.

She took it up eagerly, glad of any diversion to break the monotony of the afternoon, even if it were

only the vicar. Her eyebrows ached in surprise as she read the name: "Diana Drew, Cheyne Walk, Chelsea."

A puzzled frown creased her forehead. The name was vaguely familiar. "The young lady hopes she is not intruding," said the pontifical Parsons. "But she craves permission to make a few sketches of the exterior of the Manor 

House if you would be so kind as to allow her." 

Show her in, Parsons, and let's have tea, and tell Mrs. Hunter that we want lots and lots of crumpets."

A moment or two later Parsons ushered in Miss Drew, an attractive figure in stylishly-cut grey tweeds, plaid stockings and brogues. Dorothy made her welcome. Miss Drew seemed a little embarrassed, but with the arrival of the tea things she seemed to be more at her ease, and broached the subject of her visit.

Dorothy was a shrewd judge of character, and while chatting inconsequently enough, endeavoured to size up Miss Drew. There was something rather peculiar about her, an indefinable quality which alike intrigued and irritated.

She was a thought too much powdered and rouged, and her gestures were somewhat forced as she discussed animatedly the latest "isms" of Chelsea's 

Bohemian quarter. 66 

But my dear," dissembled Dorothy, "I'm only too delighted to show you over the Manor. I'm not frightfully keen on all this historical stuff myself, but there's a terribly interesting old priest hole hidden under the main stairway."

Miss Drew seemed much impressed and made copious notes. She spoke so interestingly of Melford's historical associations and so knowledgably of the pictures that despite herself Dorothy grew in-

terested, and was impressed by her visitor's wide experience of art and architecture. 

I say," she said, "it's been most extraordinarily interesting. Far from me showing you round, you have taught me a tremendous lot. I always thought archæology was a stuffy sort of subject, but it's quite exciting the way you put it." 

Miss Drew smiled. 

"It is the most fascinating study in the world,' she remarked, and turned as if about to make her departure.

Dorothy, with the vista of a long dull evening in front of her, said impulsively : "But won't you please stop on to dinner? It will be diner a deux, I am afraid, for both my daddy and my husband are in town." 

Miss Drew hesitated. "It's frightfully decent of you, and I should love to, but I've promised to go and see a show at the Gaiety' to-night. It was written by a friend of mine. 

I have two tickets-suppose we compromise? Let me run you up to town. 

Dorothy's eyes sparkled. "Sounds a topping idea. It's only an hour's run." 66

Half an hour later she had changed into a frock of the new daffodil yellow which promised to be all the rage. 66 'I'Il 'phone up my hubby," she remarked. "And get him to take us out to a cabaret after the show.

A faint shadow crossed over Miss Drew's face as

Dorothy moved over to the telephone and called up Bill's number. There was a pause of some minutes. 

Dorothy gave a little moue of disgust as exchange said, unemotionally : "I'm sorry, there is no reply." 

What on earth were Bill and Henry up to, she wondered.

At that moment the door opened, and Parsons announced that Miss Drew's car was waiting. Five minutes later, comfortably ensconced in the tonneau, her fur wrapped round her throat, Dorothy and her visitor were on their way to London. 

There was an odd little constrained silence between the two as they passed through Melford village and on to the main road. Dorothy turned to her companion. 66 

D'you know, I feel frightfully cold, somehow," she began, and shivered. 

66 Cold?" echoed the other. 

Then, suddenly, without warning, her arm shot out and a hand unexpectedly muscular was clapped over 

Dorothy's mouth. The attack was so utterly unexpected that for a moment or two Dorothy was too dazed to do anything-then terror seized her. The figure of Miss Drew seemed to loom gigantic above her and her arms were like steel. 

Dorothy herself was no mean athlete. She lashed out desperately, and clawed at her companion's face with her sharply manicured nails. She struggled terribly for breath. Something glittered between the other's fingers something sharp and shining in the dim glow of the tonneau light. Even as her strength ebbed away, Dorothy felt a sharp agonising pain in her left shoulder, and instantly numbness set in to her arm and her limbs grew rigid. 

Desperately she fought to marshal her swimming senses, but a great dark curtain seemed to enfold her

and with a strangled gasp she slumped forward into the all-enveloping blackness of oblivion. "Damn you, you hell cat," said the other, and the voice was shrill with anger. It was the voice of the 

Hon. Rupert Gill, one of the best amateur female impersonators in London.

He gazed down callously at the body of his inanimate victim. The side curtains had been drawn and there was no fear of discovery. The chauffeur's stolid back was as unemotional as that of a wooden effigy. "O.K., Parker," said Gill, in his shrill falsetto. "Drive like hell to Heathfield House."

CHAPTER XXXI QUIN AND THE HOUSE OF QUIET. 

THE reason why there was no reply to Dorothy's 'phone calls was not far to seek. Since the dastardly shooting attack the previous night by some of Professor Quin's hired gun-men, the gloves were off, and Bill realised that he was up against the most dangerous fight of his adventurous career.

To trace the high-powered car was impossible. Professor Quin was occupied in his private laboratory at his home in St. John's Wood.

He was a callow-looking constable, with an incipient moustache, and Quin laughed as he entered his limousine. If only the poor fool knew that he had saluted the most talked of murderer in England.

The conceit put him in a rare good humour for the short journey from St. John's Wood to Hampstead.

CHAPTER XXXII THE GAUNT WOMAN. 

How long Dorothy had remained unconscious she had no means whatever of knowing. Her tiny jewelled wrist watch had stopped, broken in the struggle with Rupert Gill. She came to herself with a sick feeling of nausea, and shivered as if with an ague. Her eyelids fluttered open and for a moment or two she could not quite make out exactly what had happened. It was if she had wakened from some fantastic nightmare. "Bill," she called out, feebly. "Bill!"

But there was no re-assuring reply: Gradually she realised that she was lying in a comfortable white bed in a white walled room smelling faintly of iodoform. 

Terrified, she half rose and strove to recover her scattered senses. Had there been an accident? This place looked like a hospital. She saw a bell push near her hand, and pressed it. She raised her hand to her throbbing forehead, and strove again to remember. Gradually, bit by bit, memory returned.

She had been going out to a show-that was itwith a woman named Hugh or Drew or something. 

Then in the car the awful creature had suddenly gone mad and had attacked her. She shivered violently, recalling that hate-distorted face leering into her own ere she lost consciousness.

What was the meaning of it all? She must get into touch with Bill at once. She heard footsteps outside the white enamelled door. For the life of her she could not stifle a shriek as the knob turned slowly, so maddeningly slowly. 

Who was at the other side of the door? Where was she? How long had she been there? These and a hundred other questions flashed instantaneously through her brain.

Slowly the door opened and a tall man in a white surgeon's apron with a stethoscope linked round his neck, entered the room. He had a square, unprepossessing face, and a square head, from which his hair stuck up bristling and forbidding. Though far from handsome, his medical appearance re-assured 

Dorothy a little. She smiled wanly. "Oh, doctor," she said, "where am I? I've been so scared. Has there been an accident? 

Dr. Saltaire bared his white, even teeth. "I'm afraid you've been very ill, young lady," he said. "You must not excite yourself." "But how long have I been here?" asked 

Dorothy, pressing a hand to her heaving breast. "For some considerable time," replied the other, non-committally. 66 In reality Dorothy had been unconscious a little over an hour. 

My-my husband," she gasped. "Have you got in touch with him? He'll be frightfully worried.

I suppose I'm in hospital, but how I got here I haven't the slightest idea." 66 No, we have not communicated with your husband-yet!" said Saltaire, and his eyes gleamed satirically. "You see, you were unconscious, and we did not quite know when you would come to." 66 

Oh, doctor, please do ring him up. His number

is Mayfair 04936. And please please may I have a drink of water?" She licked her dry lips. "Certainly. I'll send a nurse in to you at once, replied Saltaire.

He pressed a bell near the door, and a moment later a gaunt, hard-featured woman with eyes like black hat-pin heads and lips as thin and incisive as two razor blades entered the room. She was clad in conventional nurses' uniform, an incongruity that heightened the horror of her appearance. 

Dorothy shivered with repulsion as the woman approached her with her hard, unmoving black eyes. "You wanted, miss- ?" said the nurse, and her voice was metallic. "This is Mrs. Kellaway, Nurse Grimwood. She must be kept very quiet, and not be allowed to excite herself. Give her everything she requires until I finish my rounds, when I will call back and give my diagnosis. 66 66 

Yes, sir." 'You-you'll ring up my husband, won't you?" said Dorothy, faintly. "Certainly." Saltaire smiled and bowed, mockery in every lineament of his seamed square face. 

Again panic gripped Dorothy, but she managed to put on a bold front. Always she had managed to keep cool in a crisis, as Bill had long ago discovered..

CHAPTER XXXIII SYMPHONY TAKES ON A NEW JOB. 

IF Professor Hudson Quin had one defect it was that in his colossal egotism he was apt to under-rate his opponent. Bill Kellaway, like a good general, had organised his forces so as to leave as little chance of a loophole as possible. Even little Symphony Sam had his allotted task which he cheerfully fulfilled, although it was the least spectacular of the lot. 

Sam had spent the whole morning pushing a handcart complete with ladders and pail from the Italian quarter up to Hampstead.

Rejoicing in a new green baize apron and a peaked cap on which were emblazoned the words: "North- 

Western Window Cleaning Company," he arrived at Hampstead hot, tired, and thirsty. He spent a pleasant two hours at the "Green Dolphin" and somewhere about four o'clock sallied forth down 

Heathfield Avenue.

Fortunately it was not a long thoroughfare, but by the time he had finished cleaning the windows of two large suburban houses Sam had, as he expressed it feelingly, felt as if he'd "cleaned the 'ole of the 

Crystal Palace". 

It was not without quickening heartbeats that he neared Heathfield House, the scene of his nocturnal adventure, but he was a loyal little soul and he had promised his "china" Bill to see him through.

He waited with some trepidation, pail in hand, wash leathers stuck professionally in his belt. He noticed to his satisfaction that the windows of the nursing home needed attention, and when the door was opened by Doyle, the pug-faced guard, he smiled ingratiatingly.

He was given a grudging assent to proceed with his task, and whistling a gay air from "Rose Marie "

Sam proceeded to wash the windows with vim and gusto. He lingered as long as possible over the job, and kept a sharp look-out for any untoward happenings. From his vantage point at the top of the ladder he observed a lounging figure that appeared occasionally at the corner of the Avenue. Evidently it was one of Hillyard's splits but Bill Kellaway was leaving nothing to chance, hence his instructions to Sam. 

Just before six o'clock his task was concluded, and after being paid, Sam piled up his paraphernalia on the cart and with all the appearance of a man who has done a good day's work, took his departure. The dusk was falling, but that did not prevent the indefatigable Sam from trying the adjacent house. To his delight he was engaged, and it was nearly quarter to seven and it had grown dark before he finally finished.

Bill had told him to pack up at seven-thirty. With aching arms, Symphony piled up his hand-cart and

It was trudged wearily down Heathfield Avenue. his intention to leave the whole lot in the yard of the "Green Dolphin" and he was smacking his lips prefatory to a long cooling pint when he saw a grey limousine flash past and draw up before the door of

Heathfield House. 

He pressed himself against the railings until the car door opened, then craned forward eagerly. The chauffeur alighted, and also what appeared to be a slim, well-dressed woman. They seemed to support between them a limp, half-dazed girl. 

With the stealth born of long practice, Symph crept forward and in the light of the adjacent street lamp glimpsed the white, drawn face of Dorothy Kellaway. 66 

By Cripes!" said Symphony Sam.

He waited a minute or two until he had seen the three figures pass into Heathfield House, then, trundling his cart at a brisk jog-trot, he arrived breathlessly, five minutes later, at the "Green 

Dolphin ".

He stayed just two minutes to drain his tankard and then obtained permission to use the telephone.

Frantically he rang up Bill's number and swore long, lucidly and categorically when the exchange said, with maddening impersonality: "I'm sorry, reply ". no 

All right, give me the nearest taxi rank," said

Symph, and while waiting for the vehicle to arrive drained two whiskies and sodas in such rapid succession that it made the landlord blink. 

Five minutes later he was speeding westward, a fierce consuming anger in his heart. 

In the meantime Bill Kellaway had not been idle.

He had once eluded every policeman in England

by hiding for a week in gaol. This time he determined to surpass that effort and hide himself from both the police and his enemies by making himself look exactly like twenty thousand other men.

So well had he succeeded that he had actually passed within a foot of Professor Hudson Quin without being recognised. Quin was not the only one who knew something about psychology. The callow young policeman who had saluted him as he entered his Rolls-Royce that afternoon was William Kellaway, alias "Hercules, Esq."

There are twenty thousand policemen in London, and as Bill argued, one more or less would hardly be noticed. He chuckled softly as he watched the 

Professor disappear in a cloud of dust. From one of 

Slade's sleuths he had learnt something of the Professor's queer menage, and again his unerring psychological insight stood him in good stead.

He knew, for instance, that the first thing to strike a foreigner on his arrival in England is the amiability, incorruptibility, and infallibility of the London 

Bobby. Quin's staff, being entirely alien, would naturally have to report to the police station, and implanted in them would be a respect for the man in blue.

When the car had rounded the corner, Bill hitched his belt and walked majestically towards the front door of the house, humming softly: "When there's constabulary duty to be done, to be done."

He pressed the bell, and the door was opened after a few minutes by Abdul, the Egyptian, who glanced with some consternation at the tall, imposing, blue-clad figure. 

Is Professor Quin at home?" said Bill. "No, effendi," replied Abdul, shaking his fuzzy head. “H’m,” said Bill judiciously taking out his note-

book and glancing very sternly at the Egyptian, mentally blessing the ubiquitous Mr. Clarkson for providing a uniform that fitted. "Your name is Abdul, and you are an Egyptian subject?" 

Ayewah, yes, effendi," replied the other.

Bill Kellaway, who knew Arabic well (he had served in Egypt during the war), wagged an admonitory finger at the man. 66 

Why did you not register at the police station this month?" he asked, in Arabic. "But I did, effendi," gasped the manservant. "Let me see your papers," said Bill, sternly, in English. 

The man hesitated. "If you will please to step inside," he said. "My master he have all the papers but I have my stifficate." 

Bill strode solemnly into the hallway in the wake of the tall, raw-boned Egyptian, who opened a drawing-room off the right of the hall. Bill's brain worked at lightning speed. He did not know how long the Professor would be away, but he knew that once he could gain access to the laboratory he might be able to find some evidence that would incriminate Quin once and for all for his many crimes. He knew that the man's colossal vanity made him record in minutest detail for posterity his exact motives and reactions after each crime he had committed. 

Quin himself had boasted about it at the "Q" club. 

Abdul stood hesitantly at the door. "Come here a minute," said Bill. "And shut that door."

The Nubian, more like a guilty schoolboy than a grown man, shut the door and turned to face the man in blue. Even as he did so Bill acted. With a 15

terrific swing he landed a left uppercut clean on the unfortunate Nubian's jaw. The attack was so utterly unexpected that the man's head was jerked backward with an audible snick. His knees sagged under him and as he slumped forward consciousness left him. 

So quick had Bill's blow been delivered with every ounce of his strength, that he had time to catch Abdul before he crashed to the floor. 66 "Neat, damned neat," chuckled Bill. Sorry,

Abdul," he continued, gently removing the man's tarboush and laying him out carefully on the hearthrug. "But when there's constabulary duty to be done, to be done '," he hummed cheerfully. "If I know anything," he added, divesting himself of his helmet and his tunic, "the rest of the menage are down in the basement lazying around. 

Quin's bound to have a decent make-up box in his lab. I think this round's mine, Satan, my friend."

CRACK!

Bill fired again, but the shot went wide. Insensate rage had spoilt his aim, and as he stumbled forward, blinded by fury, a lean and yellow hand shot out from behind the crimson curtains and tightened about his throat, while his feet were jerked from under him by two brown and muscular arms. You forget that there is a slight but unmistakable difference in the Arabic spoken in Egypt and that of the southern Sudan. Bill's fury left him almost as suddenly as it had swept over him as Quin signalled to the Chinaman to release his hold. He stood there, shoulders heaving, chin up, defiance in his eyes. Force would avail him nothing against the fiendish four. He had to meet guile with guile, subtlety with subtlety. The desperate chance he had taken in impersonating 

Abdul had not come off. Quin had recognised him from the first, and had played with him in his usual feline fashion, and now here was this staggering news about Dorothy. 

Was it a fiendish lie? A horrible trick of Quin's to goad him into self-betrayal, or was it too hideously true?

He shrugged his shoulders fatalistically, and God knows what effort it cost him to keep his voice steady as he drawled: "I rather think a dry Martini is indicated, gentlemen. What was Ah Foo before he joined your service, Professor?" he added, fingering his throat gingerly. "Strangler-in-ordinary to the Celestial Empire?"

"You'll do, Kellaway," said Quin. "It's a pity you are troubled with a conscience. You would have been a decided acquisition to our select little Satanic circle."

CHAPTER XXXVI DOROTHY'S DESPERATE PLAN: 

DOROTHY lay back, wan and limp, on a hard flock pallet in the white ward of Heathfield House.

An hour had elapsed since the last visit of the sinister Dr. Saltaire. She lay there limp, relaxed, exhausted. To her nostrils was wafted the sickly pungent smell of iodoform, and she shivered. There is nothing more potent for revivifying memories than the sense of smell. It brought back to her vividly a childhood memory of her first visit to a hospital. 

She was to have her tonsils out, and the terror of the knife that seized her then now held her in its grip. 

The terror of the knife!

The Grimwood woman had not minced matters. 

She had been warned that an operation was due, and her soul was sick with terror.

Again and again through her tortured brain she questioned-where was Bill, dear, big, kindly Bill on whom she could lean? She felt so utterly alone.

It was the nameless uncertainty that had unnerved her. 

Desperately she tried to fight back her self-control. 

Footsteps sounded along the corridor outside, and the hard-featured nurse entered, closing the door softly behind her. 

Dorothy wanted to scream.

From her lowered lashes she studied Nurse Grimwood, and wondered whether that granite-faced woman would be amenable to bribery. There was no softness, no womanliness to which she could appeal in that grim, hard face, but gold might perhaps unlock the door where kindness and charity failed?

With an effort she sat up, and beckoned to the nurse. "Has Doctor Saltaire rung up for my husband yet?" she inquired feebly. 66 

Yes, he has, and there is no reply," answered the woman. 

Dorothy sighed. "Listen, nurse," she said, "I'm feeling better, much better. Do you think it would be possible for you to smuggle me out of here? I would pay you well. My husband is a millionaire." 

Nurse Grimwood laughed shortly. "Delusions, like the rest of them," she said. 

Dorothy's heart failed her. She was like a bird trapped in an inexorable net from which there was no escape. She made one last desperate attempt and scrabbled feebly at the door of her locker. "See, I am speaking the truth," she said. “You'll find a wrist watch in there. My husband bought it for me. It cost two hundred guineas. 

It's yours if you'll only get me out of this place."

The nurse's hands closed greedily over the little jewelled trinket, and into her hard black eyes there crept the yellow gleam of avarice. She glanced cautiously over her shoulder, then touched the side of her beaky nose. "I'd be risking prison," she said, and Dorothy's heart leapt at this first faint sign of weakening on the woman's part. "But surely, surely you can manage it?" she pleaded, urgently. "Didn't I hear Dr. Saltaire saying he was going to St. John's Wood or somewhere?" "That is so," agreed the woman reluctantly. "There's only Schwartz on duty, and he's having his dinner." "Listen," Dorothy clutched the woman's sleeve,

her pretty piquante little face drawn and intense with urgency. Get me away from here now-now. 

Surely there's some way you could smuggle me out? 

I promise you faithfully I will give you a cheque for a hundred pounds as soon as I am back with my husband. Where is this place? I have no idea where I am.' 669 

'Ampstead,' said the woman. "It's terrible risky. Apart from losing my job, there's terribly heavy penalties for letting loonies escape." "But I'm not a lunatic," said Dorothy in horror. "I tell you I was kidnapped and brought here by car by that awful woman. She must have drugged me with a hypodermic needle." ·་

Outside in the darkness Detective Sergeant Ayres, who had relieved Detective Fletcher at nine o'clock, grumbled as he flung away his cigarette and surveyed the shuttered windows of Heathfield House. He would not be relieved until three. It was a cold night, and he thought longingly of the police canteen.

Hillyard, true to his word, had placed Heathfield House under observation. Fletcher had duly reported the arrival of the grey limousine and the two young ladies. Unfortunately, while patrolling the rear of the house he had not seen Rupert Gill's departure with Professor Quin. 

Sergeant Ayres fumbled in his pocket for a cigarette and began his lonely vigil. Shortly after nine o'clock he noticed two women in nurses' uniform emerge from the side gate. They were too far away for him to hear their conversation, but judging by the letters held by the taller of the two, they were just off to the corner to catch the nine-thirty post. 

Sergeant Ayres saw nothing suspicious in the fact, and as he resumed his beat agreed wholeheartedly with W. S. Gilbert regarding the dearth of enjoyment in a policeman's life.

"Your ill-advised meddling by helping Bannerhead Bruce to escape has, to put it mildly, put us in a very awkward situation. "Now listen to me, Quin. I swore when I first joined your club that I'd put every man jack of you where you belong. You, Quin, three weeks after your trial, will take a short walk with a gentleman whom you've never met before -and you are not coming back from that walk!"

Bill spoke very slowly and deliberately, and he saw by the sudden flush of colour in Quin's sallow cheeks that his riposte had gone home. "Whether I am killed or not is entirely immaterial," continued Bill. "I have elaborated my plans, and I have seen to it that I have faithful friends who will carry them out. I regret nothing, and I forget nothing. Don't bluff," snarled Quin. "I am not bluffing," returned Bill. "If you care to verify my statement you have but to ring up.

Hovenden and Gill were visibly shaken. The calm, deliberate manner in which Bill had spoken had secured the effect he wanted to achieve. It gave them all an uneasy impression that he had several cards up his sleeve, and that he knew infinitely more than they suspected.

In reality, Bill was sparring for time. He had realised that his own situation was hopeless once his disguise as Abdul had been penetrated. For himself he did not care very much.

CHAPTER XXXVIII A DIABOLIC CROSSWORD PUZZLE. 

FOR nearly ten minutes Bill Kellaway sat on the uncomfortable steel chair in that cold, clean, and soulless cell.

He was reviewing the situation. The more he considered matters the more hopeless the impasse seemed.

The suspense was agonising. It was not so much for himself that he cared, though life had never tasted sweeter. When he had pitted his wits against the "Secret Six" he had been single, penniless, with no one to care or worry whether he came through or not.

This time it was vastly different, and he felt handicapped and shackled.

With an effort he strove to switch his mind off to other things. His gaze rested on the plaque on the wall with its peculiar definitions. What a fantastic brain Quin had! 

There was something bizarre about that checkertopped table. The innocuous crossword puzzle now took on a strange and sinister significance. What

was the idea of it all, he wondered? Certainly it was not there for ornament or amusement. Quin never did anything without a reason. 

Bill glanced down the list of definitions. A few ran as follows:

I. Across. 

Balder's murderer. I. 

8. Unpoisonable beast. 2. 

Witch's familiar. 

Prince of the Jinns. 24. 18. 20. 

25. Foe of Set. 

4. Down.

Son of Evil Morning. 

Early Biblical sinner. 

Satan's chief enemy. 

Way of escape. 

Bill scratched his chin and grinned wryly. The clues were sinister and diabolical enough. He noticed that the top of the table was ruled into seven squares across and seven down. "Seven," he repeated, half aloud.

A queerly significant number. Bill had dabbled in numerology, and wondered if there was any particular reason for the arrangement of the squares. 

It was like Quin's queer whimsical brain to fix on an esoteric seven. He groped in his pocket for a cigarette beneath his tattered gallabeyah and to his delight discovered a half-smoked butt end and a packet of book matches. Inhaling the smoke gratefully he sat down on the steel chair and gazed at the forty-nine black and white squares on the table with a frown of perplexity. 

Dorothy, hurrying through the gloom with Nurse

Grimwood, scarcely realised what she was doing. 

She was conscious only of being free. Her head still throbbed, and her heart pumped violently with excitement as they negotiated the door. "Come quickly," whispered Nurse Grimwood. "I'll pretend I'm going to post some letters."

Dorothy followed her, unresisting. Her brain was in a turmoil. 

They neared the corner of Heathfield Avenue. To 

Dorothy's ears came the hum of traffic and the familiar sounds of London by night, and she smiled wanly. It was a noise, dear and reassuring, that she had thought never to hear again.

Nurse Grimwood stopped at the pillar-box and her black beady eyes rested for a moment on Dorothy's pale weary face. "A hundred guineas, you said. I don't want it in a cheque. They'll trace cheques, and-"

A cunning, avaricious look spread over her haggard features. 

That fur coat, dearie” 'Oh take it, take it," returned Dorothy. “I'm only too grateful to you, nurse."

Re-action had set in. She had keyed herself up sufficiently for the flight, but now she felt weak and dizzy. There was not a soul about and she clutched the woman's sleeve. "Do you think you could get me a taxi?" she said, hesitantly as the rain began to drizzle from the leaden sky.

Nurse Grimwood peered into the surrounding gloom, and gave a short hard laugh. 66 

Better than that, dearie," she said. "I rang up a young man that I know who keeps a garage round here, and told him to meet us at the pillar-box. He'll take you home to your hubby, never fear." 

Dorothy looked at the woman, and smiled in gratitude. "Bless you my dear," she said.

"Yes, yes, yes," said Dorothy. Half sobbing she opened the tonneau door. In a little while she would be back with Bill, dear, big, dependable Bill.

CHAPTER XXXIX THE ZERO HOUR. 

BANNERHEAD BRUCE, as befits a good newspaperman, had always lived up to his favourite motto, "L'audace, l'audace, et toujours l'audace," which he colloquilly translated into English as Cheek, cheek, and always the bloomin' cheek." 66 

He had approached the editor of the "Daily 

Flash", the Ray's" rival, intending to use that newspaper for the purpose of exposing Hovenden. 

And now, accompanied by Henry and Symphony

Sam, who was unusually silent, they motored as far as St. John's Wood.

The car drew up at the end of Priory Road, and 

Bruce buttoned up his overcoat and smiled at his two comrades. "Don't forget, Henry, if I'm not back within an hour, get dead set for the Riot Act."

Staff of Life '." 

Bannerhead Bruce grinned, Bruce grinned, and squared his shoulders.

"So long, Henry. I will now give my celebrated impersonation of Daniel."

The newspaperman walked briskly through the rain. He patted his hip pocket and was re-assured by the cold comforting feel of his automatic. As he neared the door of the lonely house he heard a muffled roar above the hiss of the rain. "What the devil," he ejaculated, startled, as the roar was repeated, rumbling and menacing through the night. Then he remembered. There had recently been complaint in the police court against the noise made by Professor Quin's two lions in his private menagerie. He had been fined a nominal sum and had promised that the nuisance should be abated.

He rang the bell and waited, tense with expectancy, for the answer to his-summons. 

It was probably the bravest deed that Bannerhead 

Bruce had ever done in his life. A mad impulse surged over him as he waited there, it seemed for an eternity. An impulse to turn and run and get away before his summons was answered. There came the soft slither of footsteps and the door was opened a few cautious inches. He found himself gazing into the yellow impassive features of a gigantic Chinaman. 

"Is Professor Quin at home?" demanded Bruce quickly.

CHAPTER XLI FIVE LOAVES-AND TWO FISHES. 

It is a truism in the history of crime that the world's greatest criminals are more often than not brought to justice by tiny and seemingly insignificant oversights. 

Some queer imp of the perverse trips up even the cleverest criminal at the end, and with Professor

Hudson Quin, unquestionably the greatest and most audacious crook of modern times, the end came sudden and swift through an oversight that he never realised to the last.

He had planned the most stupendous crimes in the history of criminology, had welded together into a formidable weapon against society the "Q" club which was composed of the most brilliant, unscrupu lous, and dissolute degenerates in Europe. He had murdered with impunity, escaped the clutches of the law for half a lifetime, yet he was destined to fall in the end by losing his temper with a small boy and forgetting that bread in Hampstead is not delivered at night.

In sober fact it may be said of Professor Quin that he was of the unique men who died by bread alone. 

It was a somewhat awestruck and silent company that left the laboratory and followed Quin back into the library. Saltaire was there; so, also, was Dorothy 

Kellaway, wide-eyed and wan, too terrorised to move or speak. 

Quin swept her with his cold, appraising glance, and suddenly recalled the interesting experiment he was going to perform should Kellaway have proved recalcitrant.

He bowed mockingly. mockingly. She was undoubtedly pretty. He felt a queer sadistic impulse. After allwhy not? In a little while Kellaway would be out of it.

Dorothy stared around her, bewildered, terrified. "Wh-what am I doing here?" she managed to gasp. 

Quin bowed. 

My dear madame, I regret this temporary inconvenience, but it is essential in the interests of science," he remarked, blandly.
