His voice was brisk and he radiated confidence.

masonry came the strident clang, clang, clang of a fire bell.

Faint and dizzy from the searing heat and half choked with the acrid smoke that poured into his lungs, Bill held grimly on, assisting the dazed and speechless newspaperman. His eyes streamed with tears and he stumbled like a blind man. The greedy flames licked at his heels. 

Suddenly his bursting lungs felt a rush of cool air. 

He strained his bloodshot eyes and saw a black gap in what seemed to be a wall of flame. "Come on, Bruce," he said hoarsely. "We'll be out of this in a second."

Holding his breath, he half carried, half dragged the newspaperman through that crimson inferno and a moment or two later gulped in great draughts of clean night air which freshened him like wine. He swayed slightly. The sweat poured down his face. 

His eyes were dazed and blinded. His ears suddenly distinguished the deep droning zoom of an aeroplane at close quarters and a current of air swept back the hair from his forehead.

Dazedly Bill saw that he had staggered out into the garden in the rear of the house, realised also that a plane was being tuned up a hundred yards ahead. "By God, No!" he gasped. Bruce, that swine 

Quin's beating us to it. 

With a howl that would have done credit to a dancing dervish, Bill Kellaway recognised in the reflection of the crimson flames the tall figure of Professor Hudson Quin as he frantically searched beneath the fusilage for something.

The propeller stopped and the sudden silence was dramatic. 

Bill took a deep grateful breath of the night air, then with a superhuman effort raced towards the Moth.

Quin did not hear him. He straightened up and hurried into the hangar towards the limp figure of 

Dorothy. His face, in the light of the leaping flames, was truly diabolic as he crouched over the terrorstricken girl. "I've beaten them, you beauty," he rasped. "Beaten them! 66 

Have you, HELL!" said Bill Kellaway.

Quin spun round with an oath. On the threshold of the hangar, his face terrible and implacable as 

Fate itself, stood Kellaway. "You?" 

The master criminal's voice cracked with fear as

Bill advanced slowly towards him, eyes ablaze, his great hands opened to clutch. 

Keep off-keep off!" gasped Quin.

In that moment of crisis his iron nerve was giving way. He reached feverishly for his gun, then cursed as he realised that he had just donned his leather flying kit. 66 66 

Stop it, Kellaway-stop!" he babbled. 'Don't look at me like that-don't, do you hear me?" 

Bill Kellaway's lips curved into a strange and terrible smile. His voice cut like a rawhide whip. "Quin," he said. "I'm going to kill you. There is no escape. I AM THE LAW!"

Bill's tall figure seemed to loom larger and larger at the hangar's entrance. His eyes seemed to bore into the black depths of Quin's sin-soaked soul. He backed away, hands held before his face, striving to escape that terrible, implacable stare. "Keep back, damn you!" he screamed. "Don't look at me like that.” He felt helpless beneath that semi-hypnotic stare, for he read death in Kellaway's eyes. 

Suddenly Bill's great hands shot forward and he seized the Professor by the scrawny throat. A wild

primeval surge of exultation swept over him. The 

Professor squealed and the squeal died away into a choking gasp beneath that terrible stranglehold. He lashed out feebly with his feet but Bill hardly felt the savage hacks on his shins.

Slowly, inexorably, he pressed back that monstrous head. Dimly he was aware of shouting voices, clanging fire bells. He had a confused image of policemen, the blurred figure of Bannerhead Bruce and- 

There came an audible snick. The Professor's head moved limply and his body stiffened. 

Bill, appalled, let it go. The Professor crashed to the floor, his head twisted at a grotesque angle like a flower that has been snapped on its stalk.

It was in that moment that Bill realised, and realising, had no regrets. 

A smoke-grimed, wan-faced figure grinned at him approvingly from the doorway. "Hell of a good headline, Bill," said Bannerhead 

Bruce. Hanged in a Hangar!'" 666 

Bill smiled a trifle foolishly, then swayed and would have fallen had not a firm arm supported him. "A whisky after your exertions, would, I suggest, be beneficial, sir," said the voice of Henry Henry.

Bill began to laugh-weakly-hysterically. 

Grave, calm, and unflurried, Henry Henry, that amazing man, stood beside him with a silver brandy flask and a small silver cup. "Water or soda, sir?" said Henry. 

Bill did not answer. A great blackness came, but round his lips played a smile of triumph. 

Henry Henry coughed discreetly as he drew back the curtains of Bill's sitting-room and let in the sun-

light. He balanced with the dexterity of long prac tice a silver salver on which reposed two cups of tea and some hot buttered rolls.

Folded beside the teapot was a copy of the "Daily 

Flash" and the "Daily Photo". 

Henry smiled, knocked at the bedroom door, and was greeted by Bill's sleepy voice. entered. 66 

Good morning, sir.

Good morning, madam." paternal in his glance as he 

Dorothy, an adorable figure in negligee, yawned luxuriously. Bill eagerly reached for the newspaper. "Well, Henry?" he said, and his blue eyes danced. "Very well, sir," said Henry. "Mr. Bruce, considering the circumstances, has excelled himself, sir —if I might be permitted to comment." "Permitted to comment, indeed?" said Dorothy, indignantly. "Henry, you old darling, if you don't drop that pontifical tone of yours I shall-I shall kiss you so there." 

Madam, the osculatory art is one that I have not, I regret to say, practised with the assiduity which 

With a laugh Dorothy hopped out of bed and implanted a chaste kiss on Henry Henry's face. "Cunnin' devil," said Bill, with a chuckle. "Look at this, Dot," he added, pointing to the streaming headlines. ·་

"Holy Mike!" said Bill. "What a story! 

As Henry said, Bannerhead Bruce had excelled

himself.

His astounding disclosures of the inner workings of the "Q" club and of the duplicity of Baron Hovenden caused the greatest sensation that 

Fleet Street had ever known.

Bill flung the paper on one side and yawned wearily, then glanced across at Dorothy. 66 

Henry, me for a quiet life after this," he said. 

And Henry sighed a little as he noticed the adoring look in two pairs of vivid blue eyes. "Indubitably, sir. As the late Prince Albert remarked on one occasion: The practice of domestic virtues is the only true felicity'."

A tap sounded at the door and with a murmur of apology Henry crossed over to answer it.

He re- turned in a few minutes with a beautiful bouquet of fragrant roses and presented them with a bow to Dorothy. "With Mr. Nick's compliments, madam," he said. 

Dorothy buried her face in the fragrant blossoms and then glanced at the card which accompanied them. On it was scrawled in Symph's sprawling handwriting : "To deer Mrs. Nobby. Hopping they finds you as they leaves me in the pink. From Symfony." "Isn't it perfectly "The dear," said Dorothy. sweet of him?” 

Bill nodded lazily, a cigarette between his lips and his hands folded behind his head. After all, life was very good and very sweet. He had achieved his object. The "Q" club was smashed. Every single member had perished in the explosion. It was strange how the prison which Quin had intended as a death-trap had been his and Bannerhead's salvation. 

The steel-lined walls had protected them from the full force of the explosion.

Bill looked across at his friend, Henry. "It's a grand life, Henry, if you don't weaken." 

Henry Henry bowed. "Indubitably, sir. If I may quote an apposite remark of the late Prince Consort's on the occasion of the birth of Princess Beatrice, his ninth and last child- All's well that ends well'. Trite, perhaps, -but true." 

Henry Henry deftly fielded the pillow thrown at him by the irate Bill. Dorothy's silvery laugh filled the room.

THE END
